#6982. What Lies Beneath the Noise

In 1980, I made the first big error of my political-writing career by not trusting my intuition. As we approach historic midterms, I won’t make the same mistake.

Some days, I wonder how I ended up in this fun-house mirror of a timeline. I came to understand about midway through the 2016 general-election campaign that almost everything I knew about American politics was wrong. When the results from 2024 began to roll in, I decided that whatever I still thought I knew about American politics was also wrong. Which prompted me to recall one of the worst mistakes I ever made in this plague-ridden craft of mine.

It was 1980, and I was covering the presidential campaign between Ronald Reagan and Jimmy Carter for The Boston Phoenix. (While I am not at the moment dead, Reagan, Carter, and the Phoenix all are.) As part of our coverage of the homestretch, the newspaper rented me a car and sent me on the road to take the temperature of what was still charmingly called the New Deal coalition. I was to drive to various stops throughout the newly deindustrialized Midwest and see how the children of the people who’d benefited from the New Deal itself were doing. My first stop was in Youngstown, and the first—and last—answer was “not well at all.”

I stopped by a steelworkers union hall to talk to the president of a local and to some of the members. On the way, I passed closed steel mills, vast and faceless and empty beyond empty. Bits of rusted metal flaked off the plant buildings and drifted on the breeze like dirty snow in October. The crowd in the union hall was sparse and grim. The first thing I noticed was that every bit of furniture was marked with numbers written in chalk. Every chair. Every desk. Every file cabinet. I asked the local president whether they were moving. He said no. That Saturday, they were going to have a giant garage sale, and those numbers were the prices on the various items. They were selling off the union hall piecemeal. This should have told me something. In fact, it did, but I forgot about it.

The situation didn’t get better on the rest of the trip. I drove from Youngstown to Toledo, up to Flint, and finally to Grand Rapids, where I saw President Carter visit a local library. He sat in a chair that was much too small for him. And I stood in a narrow aisle taking notes and feeling as if I had walked into one of those miniatures that Toni Collette makes in Hereditary before she loses her head. Everything around me looked small, including the president of the United States. This should have told me something. In fact, it tried, but I was deaf to the obvious.

When I finally got to Milwaukee, I settled into an empty classroom at my old journalism school to write my magnum opus. I thought about the people selling off the union hall in Youngstown and about the weird dollhouse town meeting in Grand Rapids, and I pounded out the following passage on a borrowed gray Royal upright:

“The New Deal is as dead as the steel industry. Jimmy Carter is going to lose, and he is going to lose in a landslide. It is entirely possible that he will fail to receive 100 electoral votes.”

At the time, all the elite opinion agreed that the electorate was “undecided.” The overwhelming consensus was that the American voter was a muddled, disillusioned mess, liable to jump in any direction, including over a cliff. And I walked right into a rookie mistake. I removed my “landslide” theme and went with the “muddled America” theme. On election night, I gathered with my fellow alternative journos at a friend’s house. It was the angriest collection of sullen souses you’ve never seen. The results rolled in, and it plainly was a late-arriving catastrophe for the Democratic party. Carter lost state after state. He even lost Massachusetts. Giants of the Senate—Frank Church, George McGovern, Gaylord Nelson—all sank out of sight. And all night long, that lost paragraph rang like a gong in my memory.

I’d seen it. It was right there in front of my eyes. It was there in the chalked-up office furniture and in the shrunken presence of the incumbent president, who I’d once written would not get 100 electoral votes and ended up with 49. Not that my readers knew any of this, because I had lost faith in my own observations and my own instincts. I learned a valuable lesson that night: Trust what you know and what you see, because sometimes huge changes are under way, beneath all the vain noises that signify nothing at all.

Our revels now are ended. These our actors,

—Shakespeare, The Tempest, Act IV, Scene I

The 2026 midterm campaigns, which may indeed be the country’s last exits from an onrushing nightmare, have entered the realm of the spirits. Phantom issues abound. White House aides in the trunks of cars. With which podcaster have you appeared, and why haven’t you denounced them yet? Dead campaigns haunt the living ones. These are things that will lose all substance in our politics sometime on November 4.

Over the past few months, the Democratic party, the wind at its back to begin with, has demonstrated a remarkably energetic intellectual and ideological diversity. New candidates with a startling variety of appeals to a startling variety of constituencies have popped up everywhere from Alaska to Florida. Money is flowing to all of them for different reasons. Ordinarily, this would be a cause for institutional joy, if not perilous giddiness.

However, in the realm of the spirits, nothing is as it seems. This diversity of appeals to diverse constituencies scares the daylights out of some Democrats. It’s expressed as a party wrestling with internal contradictions and with a collision of generations. But I would argue that it really represents the last, fading tremors of the fear that entered the Democratic party when it awoke the morning after that dark day in 1980 and realized that its New Deal coalition had collapsed under its feet without anyone noticing.

Out of this came the ceaseless pursuit of white working-class men who were as gone forever from the party as their factory jobs were from places like Youngstown. The Republicans offered these folks someone to blame, which is precisely the crack cocaine they were seeking. Unfortunately, these also were the most reliable voters the Democrats had. The involuntary flinching reflex among the national Democrats proved very hard to unlearn. The available evidence so far this year is that the long process may have finally come to completion.

The Democrats come into November with a variety pack of very good candidates. In Massachusetts, Senator Edward Markey has fended off another challenge. Markey entered Congress in 1976, which was 13 years before James Talarico, the senatorial candidate in Texas, was born. Sherrod Brown in Ohio is trying to return to the Senate, while Abdul El-Sayed up in Michigan is trying for the Senate for the first time.

And then, all the way out in the 14th Congressional District of California, a woman named Aisha Wahab has replaced the disgraced Eric Swalwell in the House of Representatives. She is a Muslim. She was born to Afghan refugees. And now she is the first Afghan American elected to Congress and is favored to stay there after the midterms.

Wahab has joined a whole clutch of congresscritters who served in Afghanistan. With any luck at all, they will all talk to each other and learn something that will help this country make better choices in that part of the world. The power of change, beneath all the vain noises.

#6980. Quince Cashmere Review: The Affordable Sweaters Cosmo Editors Swear By

We tried Quince’s cult-favorite knitwear to see which styles live up to the hype.

To me, few pieces of clothing feel quite as luxurious as a cashmere sweater—what can I say, I have expensive taste! I love how cashmere feels soft against my skin, keeps me warm without making me sweat, and elevates my cold-weather outfits. But what I don’t love? The often-astronomical price point. That’s what put me on to Quince, the brand made famous for its $50 cashmere crewneck.

Our editors have been test-driving Quince’s cashmere sweaters for years, and we’ve fallen in love with their elegant, high-quality designs that we reach for on the daily (not to mention, they make for great gifts). #6979. Esquire First Look: Pedro Pascal and Tony Gilroy Duet in the Musical Drama Behemoth!

Pedro Pascal shares how he became a cello prodigy in Tony Gilroy’s drama about sex, orchestras, and being the ultimate player.

“You can’t hide in this room,” Tony Gilroy says.

The Andor creator and Michael Clayton filmmaker is standing amid rows of orchestra musicians in the center of the historic Newman Scoring Stage on the 20th Century Fox lot in Los Angeles, California. It’s where the music for Jaws, The Sound of Music, and countless other classic films was recorded. But on this day, the recording session is part of the actual movie. Pedro Pascal straddles a cello among the dozens of co-stars—who are actual violinists, cellists, French horn players—as they all shoot the opening scene of the musical drama Behemoth!

Gilroy points at the ornate web of microphones dangling from above, and then to the glassed-in control room where a team of engineers are attuned to every whisper. “When that red light goes on, there’s 23 people listening in that room,” he says. “You can hide in a philharmonic. If you’re off. If you’re sick. A concert keeps going and doesn’t stop.”

But here, in this historic space, the precision equipment doesn’t just expose every false note—it records each one for posterity. This is where Alex Serian, Pascal’s virtuoso cellist, discovers he can’t hide anymore. His past, going way back to childhood, is littered with broken promises and false hopes. His love life is a freeway pile-up. His family has disintegrated. All he has is the cello, and now it’s literally time to face more than the music.

“The music is pulling him right from the very beginning of it, pulling him forward to this destiny, pulling him to do everything,” Gilroy tells Esquire during our exclusive set visit. By focusing on the one thing he is good at, the only thing he can execute flawlessly, Alex finds a way to revisit all these broken memories and figure out a new way forward. He’s already a world-class musician, but he’s trying hard to become a better man.

Pascal says Gilroy explained it like Alex was being swept along in a flood. The actor gets choked up as he describes this lost person, grasping for something to hold onto and pull himself out. “He's on a river,” Pascal says. “I think that the musical notes on the page can be the rope.”

The Newman Scoring Stage is usually off-limits to the public. It’s not an exquisitely styled concert hall. It’s an open space big enough to be a basketball court, but not big enough for spectators. The wood panels affixed to the walls look like they’re in someone’s unfinished basement, ready for plaster and a paint job. It’s named after nine-time Oscar-winning composer Alfred Newman, who won awards for 1939’s Wuthering Heights, 1950’s All About Eve, and 1970’s Airport, released the year he died. It was renamed for him in 1997, but this is the first time the Hollywood workspace has been one of the stars of a movie itself.

In the film, out on December 4, Pascal’s character has abandoned the world of symphonies and concerts and has returned to his hometown of Los Angeles, where he finds work as a session musician working on movie scores.

Behemoth! doesn’t have one single composer—it has nine. Each of them contributed themes for the various fictional films that hire Alex as a performer. Henry Jackman, best known for Kick-Ass and Captain America: Civil War, penned the tense tones of the made-up Spies Don’t Run, which you can hear a sample of below.

Among the other contributors are Back to the Future composer Alan Silvestri, Marvel Studios and Pixar mainstay Michael Giacchino, Moana 2 co-writer and Beyoncé pianist Emily Bear, Get Out and Nope maestro Michael Abels, and The Hunger Games and Batman Begins composer James Newton Howard. The film also features composers Lukas Frank (singer-songwriter with the band Storefront Church), Nami Melumad (Star Trek: Strange New Worlds) and Brandon Roberts (an Emmy-winner for Free Solo). Each contributes a different flavor to the story, underscoring the fact that Behemoth! is sometimes a romance, sometimes a family drama, sometimes a farce, and occasionally even a cartoon.

As Alex plays the music for these various movies, he unlocks more memories from harrowing times: His late composer father (Hank Azaria) pushed him to extremes before exiting the stage and leaving it all unresolved. Now Alex must face the gradual decline of his harp-playing mother (JoBeth Williams), who is battling Alzheimer’s disease. He has a loving but fraught relationship with his brother (Will Arnett), a rockstar turned podcaster, who—if it’s not already clear from that description—has fallen on hard times.

Then there are the women in his life, which he has helped make into a symphony of disorder. Olivia Wilde plays a ferociously jilted ex who also happened to be a major symphony benefactor—and can’t abide her favorite player moving on. Margarita Levieva is an old friend and sometimes lover who reopens old wounds, and Eva Victor is a fellow musician he once cared for deeply, who abandoned him with a head full of unanswered questions.

“The one language he understood best was music. So that's just something that he simply had to cling to,” Pascal says. “Now, the second language that he is probably better at is lovemaking—and not in any kind of Casanova way, but simply as a pure form of existing and expressing himself and being connected. The rest makes less sense to him. It’s so hard for him to navigate using words to express how he's feeling. It's all in his playing.”

Gilroy says it was vital to cast someone like Pascal, who could bring a sense of wounded innocence to Alex. “He's obviously damaged by this toxic family that he came from,” Gilroy says. “He's not a predator in any way, but he's a very sensual person and a lot of women have been attracted to him.” He adds with a laugh: “And he's really easy.”

But none of this has given Alex the comfort and stability he seeks. “I get a little emotional because it's such a subtle thing that Tony's done,” Pascal says. “It’s healing in a way that Alex isn't completely conscious of, but he’s led by the language of music and starts to apply that language to his life."

On the day Esquire visited the set last January, it was also the first time Alex has this awakening. They were using the Newman Scoring Stage as the setting for the film’s very first sequence, in which Alex plays cello as part of a pulse-pounding session for Spies Don’t Run. This is an in-joke for Gilroy, who wrote all the Jason Bourne films and directed The Bourne Legacy. Consider it self-referential, but far from reverential.

Behemoth! pokes fun at Hollywood tropes while paying tribute to the unseen men and women whose music often make films stronger than they might be otherwise. “I've been obsessed with studio musicians since I was 19,” Gilroy says. “I came out to L.A. to try to be a studio musician, and I realized how deficient I was within three days of playing guitar.”

Around the recording stage, there are posters for the made-up Spies movie, but the face of the dark-haired, suave secret agent—billed as Dominic Diaz above the title—is obscured. During a break between set-ups, Pascal asks Gilroy: “Who is the equivalent of Dominic Diaz?” He flashes his eyebrows up and down. “Is it Pedro Pascal?”

Those eyebrows are furrowed a few minutes later as he consults with Timothy Loo, who trained the actor on how to believably play the cello. Pascal doesn’t claim to be on the same level, but now he can believably fake it. “I can do a terrible, terrible ‘Twinkle, Twinkle Little Star,’” he says.

After watching playback of a previous sequence, Loo says: “Your left hand looked great.”

Pascal shakes his head. “The bow got away from me. I don’t know what happened. But it won’t happen again.”

The two had a history even before they began intensive training for Behemoth!, since among Loo’s hundreds of credits is the contemplative cello from Pascal’s own corner of the Star Wars universe. “He's a kind of king amongst musicians for film in Los Angeles,” the actor explains later. “I had met so many of them and had been in the same room years earlier when Ludwig Göransson was scoring the first season of The Mandalorian.”

That happened to be right here, in the Newman Scoring Stage. This is another place where Behemoth! crosses over from real life to fiction and back again. Alex has his own personal history with this room, dating back decades. It’s where his trumpet-playing bandleader grandfather dropped dead after recording a jazzy, silly single (co-written by Bear and Gilroy.) It’s also where he first heard music in the womb. “His mother cut sessions here when she was pregnant with him. He's been on that stage prenatally,” Gilroy says. “There's no moment in his life where music isn't his fuel, or his oxygen. There's no part of his life where it isn't just a fundamental presence. But then what?”

That’s the harder question that the movie expects Alex to answer. “He happens to be a prodigy,” says Gilroy. “He happens to be brilliant at it, but you see a lot of things that have fallen away in his life, that have gone nowhere,” the filmmaker adds. “That’s been the way of his life the whole way through.”

Finally, he’s figured out a way to make music not just a refuge, but a way to guide himself out of this messy life he’s made. It’s a backwards way of living, but that’s fitting too.

Behemoth! was put together in a backwards way. The usual method is to shoot it and edit it first. Composers might be working on various themes in advance, but after they have a working print, they study its cuts while penning the final score to make sure the music cues hit at just the right moments. Gilroy and his team turned that approach around.

The finished film is a sort of remake. Gilroy filmed rough versions of some sequences with his cellphone camera and stitched those together so the composers could write their scores with precision before principal photography began.

Once cameras were rolling for real, he had to capture the footage in an unusually merciless way. Camera movements were timed to the exact second so they would match the length and crescendos of the pre-written music. For instance, as Pascal played along with the orchestra on the Spies Don’t Run theme, a camera rolled through the row of performers to end in a close-up of him, right before a cut to his memory of an intense, wintertime confrontation with Wilde’s obsessed lover.

That meant cinematographer Damián García, a veteran of Andor, had to get his camera crew to mimic the surrounding musicians, making sure every movement happened in perfect sync. After several tries in which they were just a hairsbreadth off of the Spies roll-up, Damian tells the camera operator: “Start six seconds later and move 10 percent slower.” That turns out to be the right tempo.

This precision is a hallmark of Gilroy’s storytelling. “I've been accused—and justifiably so—of being obsessed with people who are really good at things,” he says. “You can go back and look through the credits. Other than Dolores Claiborne, I'm trying to think if there’s a movie where everybody isn't the best at something. There's some aspect of that that must personally appeal to me, that makes me satisfied.”

IMDb backs him up. From Andor and Jason Bourne, to his Oscar-nominated writing and directing of Michael Clayton and even popcorn-fests like The Devil’s Advocate and the 1992 ice-skating romcom The Cutting Edge, Gilroy has a fascination with expertise. With Behemoth!, Gilroy explores what happens when being brilliant at one thing makes everything else fall short.

Expertise can help you see the world more clearly, but it can also help you hide from it. That’s what Pascal’s character in the film comes to see in his return to the movie music scene. Behemoth! isn’t necessarily autobiographical, but Gilroy says he feels a connection to that. “If you took everything away, I have the feeling I would still be able to go into a room and write my way out of it, and I think there is some satisfaction in that. But I don't think it protects you from everybody else.”

It’s the “everybody else” that Pascal’s Alex finally has to confront. The rest of his life may be out of tune, but this is the story of a man seeking balance. You might even call it harmony.

#6976. Tom Hardy's Favorite Clothing Brand Just Made Sailing Gear Using Bulletproof-Vest Technology

Founded by two former Special Forces soldiers, British label ThruDark teamed up with Musto to make some seriously hardcore stuff.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Tom Hardy has always looked like a man who knows his way around a bit of extreme weather. So it's perhaps unsurprising that he's a fan of ultra-tough British outdoor clothing brand ThruDark. He recently set the internet on fire when he wore the brand's Rōnin Tanto ski jacket in the music video for his latest foray into rap, complete with a rather menacing balaclava. (He's actually been involved with the company since 2018, when he narrated its launch film.)

Now ThruDark has teamed up with Musto, the grand old name in British sailing gear, to produce a collection that looks ready for an Atlantic crossing—or possibly a covert military operation in the dead of night.

Founded in 2018 by former Royal Marines and Special Boat Service operators Anthony “Staz” Stazicker and Louis Tinsley, ThruDark makes seriously technical clothing informed by its founders' combined 28 years of military experience. Musto, meanwhile, has been keeping sailors dry since the ’60s. Its founder, Keith Musto, won an Olympic silver medal in 1964.

Their new Nautikos collection combines the two companies' expertise in what Musto claims is its most technically advanced offshore range yet. There's Gore-Tex Pro waterproofing, Dyneema reinforcement (an exceptionally strong fibre also used in climbing ropes, bullet-resistant armor, and yacht rigging), removable pockets that transfer between layers, impact-absorbing knee protection, and reflective details designed to help keep you visible in a storm.

The headline piece is a $2,200 waterproof jacket, with matching trousers for $1,750. There's also a $325 fleece, a $105 cap and, rather brilliantly, a $105 T-shirt made using fabric technology that incorporates recycled coffee grounds.

And what does Hardy make of it? Well, there's no suggestion he's taken up competitive sailing just yet. But the relationship with ThruDark is genuine.

“We always love to see Tom wearing ThruDark,” co-founder Stazicker tells Esquire. “From our side, we're grateful he does. It's a genuine relationship built on mutual respect, and we never take it for granted. We're really proud of what we've built with Musto, and we'd love to see it out there on anyone who puts our kit through its paces. He'd certainly wear it well.”

Whether you're planning to cross the Southern Ocean or just want to look as though you're seriously preparing for the next big nor’easter, it's an impressive amount of engineering to have on your side.

#6974. Ben Ahlers Teases Jack and Bridget’s “Tangible, Objective Obstacle” in The Gilded Age Season 4

Does entering his gentleman era mean leaving her behind?

The rise of Jack Trotter—or as the internet calls him, “Clock Twink”—has been one of The Gilded Age’s most rewarding storylines. He started as a humble footman in the Van Rhijn household and got rich practically overnight after inventing an affordable alarm clock—all while somehow avoiding becoming a terrible person. He is one of the easiest characters on the show to root for. Jack could do practically anything onscreen and I would be thrilled.

His will-they-won’t-they relationship with Bridget, one of the Van Rhijn household’s maids, is part of that. Fans have been hoping for these two to get together for multiple seasons now. But after a close call last season, they seem further apart than ever. The actor behind Jack, Ben Ahlers, chatted with Cosmopolitan about what season 4 has in store for this maybe-couple and whether Jack will be able to stay true to himself as he climbs the social ladder.

What's so interesting about this whole world is the public and private self, and Jack has become a master of that. He has a private self downstairs in the Van Rhijn household as the trickster, the one who can poke at Armstrong or keep the vibe going when everybody's having a long day. My accent would change between the two, upstairs and downstairs, over the last few seasons. But now he's in a completely different scenario where he doesn't have that family. He doesn't have the private self. As he's stepping into these new worlds with inherently manipulative, distrustful, power-hungry people, there's a level of caution.

You don't become a gentleman overnight. You don't make 300 grand and just become a swaggy dude. There's an acclimating that has to happen, and I really enjoyed playing with that tension. A lot of what you see of him over this season is him trying to find the people and the places where he can reconnect with that beautiful, loving private self.

I think he has an opportunity to more so than anybody because he is carving his own path. He was an orphan who grew up in servitude and then elevated into the upper class. There's no one else who has that track. He has the money and the skill set to provide a life for himself. But he doesn't have the support of Banister patting him on the back to tell him to keep going. It’s going to be a challenge. This isolation and new beginning is a really incredible curriculum for the type of man that I know he can be.

Jack and Bridget have one of the most foundational relationships in his life. It's been a bit nebulous. They were kind of romantic, and then they were friends, and then there are glimmers of maybe something happening at the end of season 3. It's a really unique but relatable challenge that when you're in an interpersonal relationship with that much love, how do you foster change in one another, necessary growth when that doesn't always align? Now they're in two separate classes, and that's going to create a tangible, objective obstacle. And I'm really hopeful for them to find that sense of intimacy and connection, regardless of the sort of stations of their lives.

It's so fun to play in that tension, especially with a great scene partner, because you get to try out different tactics and you get to try it at a different event or a party, and sometimes you connect and then it breaks away again. That's what makes people gravitate towards stories like this, because that expansion contraction internally and interpersonally is the whole bag that all of us are going through every day of our lives.

#6971. Kim Kardashian Gives New Life to Elizabeth Taylor’s Iconic Emerald-and-Diamond Bulgari Necklace

The dazzling piece was gifted to Taylor on her 30th birthday

If there’s one thing Kim Kardashian loves, it’s an Old Hollywood icon. There was, of course, the time she wore Marilyn Monroe’s “Happy Birthday, Mr. President” dress to the Met Gala (one of many nods she’s made to the blonde bombshell). But her references extend further, to other classic figures of the silver screen, like Audrey Hepburn, Grace Kelly, Sophia Loren, and Lauren Bacall. Still, her biggest muse may be Elizabeth Taylor.

In a new Instagram video, Kardashian documented a trip to the Bulgari store in Rome, Italy, where she was given the opportunity to try on the emerald-and-diamond necklace that was gifted to Taylor by Richard Burton on her 30th birthday.

Joined by her mother, Kris Jenner, Kardashian is seen in the clip trying on the pendant and its matching earrings, both of which she later wore for a photoshoot that was organized by her stylist, Law Roach, as a part of the ongoing All’s Fair season two press tour.

As she talks with the Bulgari associates, Kardashian reveals that she actually got the chance to see this necklace in person immediately following Taylor’s death. “When she passed away, I was brought into her home [and] shown her safe with all of her jewelry before they cleared it out,” Kardashian says.

“So, I was able to see everything in her home and her jewelry still in her safe and all of her stuff still [there], her furniture. I was able to go in there like right after she passed,” she continues. “I know that sounds insane, but I was able to see this stuff in her purple closet.”

Taylor has seemingly long been an inspiration for Kardashian, who, just last year, wore the late actor’s Lorraine Schwartz 15-carat diamond and platinum earrings while walking in Demna’s final couture show for Balenciaga.

Prior to Taylor’s passing, she and Kardashian even sat down together for a joint conversation with Harper’s Bazaar, where the Skims founder declared: “I am a huge Elizabeth Taylor nerd.”

Talk about a pinch-me moment. In the video, Kardashian even calls Law Roach to thank him. She says, “But this is like the best birthday present ever… Now I can start my year with this Elizabeth energy.”

#6960. Mckenna Grace Is a Bigger ‘Hunger Games’ Fan Than You

The actor has a way of approaching everything dialed up to 100: her work, her relationships, her fangirl enthusiasm. With Sunrise on the Reaping, it really paid off.

Mckenna Grace is operating on two hours of sleep when she opens her Zoom window. Fresh off a night shoot, she is losing her voice and has the beginnings of a black eye from taking an accidental knee to the face. She uses the camera as a mirror to check for damage, joking, “I’m so thankful that this is what I get to do and have my butt kicked on.”

She goes on to enthusiastically detail how her days on set have been filled with snake wranglers, poison ivy, and feral kittens found in the woods. It’s all very Hunger Games–coded and very fitting, of course, considering Mckenna stars in the upcoming Sunrise on the Reaping as Maysilee Donner, a snarky tribute from the fictional world of Panem’s District 12 (Mckenna also plays Maysilee’s twin sister, Merrilee, in the movie).

It’s an adventure and not one without risk, but she is very much here for it, having spent most of her life throwing herself—literally—into the universes she loves. From Scream to Ghostbusters to The Hunger Games, Mckenna has been working on her favorite franchises since she was 5 years old (“I grew up on sets, out of a suitcase,” she shares). Her IMDb page is already longer than a CVS receipt, with eight credits in 2025 alone, several in 2026, and the soon-to-premiere Netflix series Scooby-Doo: Origins, in which she plays Daphne. (If you’re curious about just how big a fan of that project Mckenna is, she named her dog, who she’s posing with on her Cosmopolitan cover, Scooby Doo and her cat Scrappy Mew.)

The enthusiasm bleeds into her relationships, too, and comes through in how she speaks about her boyfriend, her Regretting You costar Mason Thames (“my best friend”), and her Sunrise on the Reaping costar Joseph Zada, with whom she spent many nights playing chess while shooting in a remote village in Spain (“I beat him constantly. Put it in print”). Because one thing about the 20-year-old is crystal clear: Whatever she decides to tackle, she’ll be going all in.

The one thing it would require is Mckenna Grace. No, but I’m just out here clawing my way through, trying to get jobs and do things I’m proud of. It’s scary because unlike in the ’90s or early 2000s—where your favorite actors did the same thing in a bunch of movies—now with social media, people get really sick of seeing people really fast. It’s like, “This is the hot new commodity, the new face we’ve been wanting.” Three weeks later, everybody is hating on them.

The internet is a big part of the industry but very fickle. There was a time where people wouldn’t give you an interview because you had less than 3 million followers.1 Yet it’s a catch-22 because if you’re too present…but, hey, I’m just thankful to be here. And lately, I’ve been blessed to be able to do these franchises that I’m a huge fan of. I am the biggest Hunger Games fan.

1. Mckenna has a total of 13+ million social media followers.

I was very invested in all of it—the headcanons and the fan base. I had this huge Hunger Games poster. I got a bow and arrows so that I could be like Katniss. I told [director] Francis Lawrence when I got to set: “Dude, I’ve watched these movies and read these books a million times. I’ve edited these cornucopias. To be running off my own podium toward the cornucopia, this is not real to me.”

Why did it have to get back to Josh [Hutcherson] is my thing. I played it so cool for a year and a half of knowing him, and he never brought it up. But everyone keeps reminding him. Just let it die; let a girl have some dignity. I do regret it in a way, but I am also just like, “I am a real fan, a fangirl. I’m a CapCut warrior.”

I keep a journal for my characters with pages dedicated to fan things. For Maysilee, I’m like, Okay, they want to see the braid, so I’d pull Francis aside, like, “Look, man, she needs this braid in her hair; she needs this pin.” He’s like, “Sure, Mckenna.”

She does get a bad rap, and she has that snark to her, but she has a kind heart. She cares about the people she loves.

You think I’m going to slap Glenn Close? Hell no. I am terrified of her in the best way. I love her to death.

At this point, I definitely don’t do nudity or very hypersexual things. I respect anybody who can, but it’s terrifying to me. I don’t know if that’ll ever change. It’s not like I only do wholesome things—my character literally gets gutted by Ghostface in Scream—but my dream is to try and be a Disney princess someday.

As a kid on set, you’re expected to act like an adult until you’re on camera. Then you’re a kid until everybody is mad at you because you’re acting like a kid. Now it’s cool to be an adult and be able to give my opinion and have a bit more ownership over characters. I’ve really enjoyed sitting down with producers and pitching all my ideas.

I always wanted to go to a school dance. When prom season came around, I told all my friends who were single, “I don’t care where you are, I will come to your prom.” I ended up going to three proms. I didn’t know the people there. I just showed up, had a good time, and then dipped.

Your girl is happy. I’m feeling very lovey-dovey. I have somebody I can be a complete nerd with. I got my best friend, and so I don’t see the point in not loving loudly.2

2. Mckenna posted a birthday tribute in July to her boyfriend, Mason Thames, singing “honeybee” by Olivia Rodrigo. One commenter wrote, “This couple is the Gen Alpha Tom Holland and Zendaya.”

There are moments that are just for you and your person. But I don’t see the point in not celebrating who you’re in love with. A lot of people’s hesitation around this stems from the idea that if you post about someone a bunch and then you break up, that’s embarrassing. But why would I even think about that? I’m just going to focus on the present.

It’s a terrifying thing in a relationship to surrender yourself to that thought of, I’m just going to be at my most vulnerable and trust you, and this part of my life is fully in your hands. But I don’t see the point in building a relationship based on fear. I am the biggest fan of the person I’m with, so why wouldn’t I be like, “This is my person. I am so stoked I have this”? We’re always on each other’s sets anyway. He’s like an honorary member of District 12 at this point.

I hope to still be as excited waking up for work every day, even though it’s tiring and I get bruised and half the time people may hate me for things I can’t control. I’m thankful to still be feeling that way 15 years in, so I hope that 25 years in, I feel the exact same way.

3. For her birthday, Mckenna was supposed to go to the aquarium with her parents and Mason. Her shooting schedule ran too late, so her mom and Mason waited up to surprise her in shark onesies instead.

If I’m 80 and people are like, “Oh, you’re the girl from The Hunger Games,” I’ll be like, “Yes, I am. I did that. That’s so cool.”

#6958. The Low-Profile Sneaker Trends Defining Fall 2026

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Slim-sole mania is here to stay, but these looks are far from tired.

Of all the popular footwear trends in recent years, few have proved as enduring as the low-profile sneaker. And ever since iconic silhouettes such as the Adidas Samba and the Onitsuka Tiger Mexico 66 helped spark the craze, the category has seemed to evolve nearly every season. Lately, soles are slimmer than ever, with dancewear-inspired jazz flats and scrunchy, second-skin sneakers taking center stage. Bold accents like double laces and metallic finishes only add to the many ways these sleek kicks are being reimagined, making it easy to liven up even your simplest outfits.

To keep you up to date on the latest low-profile sneaker trends, we’ve gathered eight key looks below, pulled from recent runways and embraced by street style mavens and A-list celebrities alike. Read on for the full list, plus some of our favorite pairs for nailing each aesthetic.

Perhaps the slimmest interpretation of the low-profile phenomenon, jazz shoes are one of this year’s biggest breakout trends. As seen on fall 2026 runways from Celine, Jacquemus, and Tod’s, classic lace-ups offer a little extra support. Sneaker-like details such as rubbery soles and foam cushioning underfoot take the comfort level up a notch. And for something slightly sportier (and more substantial), try a cool split-sole pair like Adidas’s Taekwondo Mei Elite or Keds’s famous Champion Low in smooth leather.

Add instant pizazz to your closet this season with a pair of animal print sneakers. In keeping with an understated silhouette, try leaning into a streamlined or darker-hued print—whether snakeskin, leopard, deer, or tiger—that avoids veering too flashy.

Largely credited with popularizing the slim-soled, double-lace sneaker back in 2014, Miu Miu continues to release variations of the striking fan-favorite—including color-blocked designs on its latest spring/summer 2027 runway with yet another New Balance collaboration. And dozens of other brands are following suit with their own takes. Think not only contrasting laces stacked atop one another but also dual laces strung side by side and even a second lace layered across the entire shoe, à la brands like Vans and Puma.

A low-profile silhouette offers a decidedly elevated way to dip your toe into the metallic sneaker waters. For fall 2026, Cecilie Bahnsen’s silver-trimmed pairs felt especially balletic, teamed with cozy leg warmers, while even Shiatzy Chen’s bright red style seamlessly complemented sharp leather trousers and a coordinating fur-trimmed coat.

There’s something unmistakably cool about black sneakers, and they’re more of a staple than you might expect. They’ll add gravitas to looks that traditional white pairs can’t, while meshing perfectly with this season’s deeper palette. Appearing across autumn runways at Jil Sander, Patou, and Tod’s, simple noir lace-ups prove an equally sophisticated alternative to flats, easily styled with everything from baggy jeans to polished knitwear.

Though gorpcore has long been dominated by chunky, trail-inspired sneakers, some brands are making the case for slightly slimmer treatments—in a somewhat similar vein to those controversial Vibram FiveFingers adopted by the street-style crowd. On its fall/winter 2026 runway, Loewe debuted a swim-shoe hybrid, while Lacoste drew upon the idea of a tennis ball hitting a net to create a barely-there, caged sneaker for spring/summer 2027. For a nice switch-up from the usual lineup, try a snug slip-on or low-profile style with hearty overlays or a rubber toe bumper.

Vibrant yellow sneakers don’t belong solely in summer. Designers at Fendi, Loewe, Cecilie Bahnsen, and Onitsuka all debuted versions for the fall/winter season, providing a serotonin-boosting jolt of color against fall staples in muted tones.

Falling in line with the nostalgic return of scrunched heels and flats, slim, elasticized sneakers are all the rage right now. Prada jump-started the trend with its now-viral Collapse sneaker for spring/summer 2025, and the style returned in multiple iterations on both of its 2026 runways. Meanwhile, Nike’s Moon Shoe collaboration with Jacquemus continues to be a hit, as spotted on Rihanna earlier this year. Other satin favorites, like Miu Miu’s beloved Plume sneakers and Puma’s latest ruched-toe H-Street Etoile, offer an even daintier spin on the comfy aesthetic.

Any content created in partnership with advertisers is marked as such.

#6954. Maya Hawke’s Guide to Joining a Global Fandom (Again)

The Stranger Things and Inside Out 2 alum thought she knew what it was like to be a part of a major franchise. Then came Sunrise on the Reaping.

It’s Maya Hawke’s day off. But you wouldn’t know it based on what she’s done so far: a few hours of making flash cards for an upcoming project followed by a full slate of meetings, one per hour. Now we’re at dinner in New York City, talking about her role as the brilliant, unconventional victor Wiress in The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping.

Stepping into a beloved pop culture franchise is nothing new for the 28-year-old multi-hyphenate (she played both the smart, sarcastic Robin Buckley in Stranger Things and the voice of the perpetually frazzled Anxiety in Inside Out 2). Neither is existing in a curious state of constant motion. In the past year alone, Maya has released a folk-pop album, Maitreya Corso; filmed and appeared in multiple TV and film projects; served as a Prada brand ambassador; and gotten married to singer-songwriter Christian Lee Hutson.

Yet beneath the impressive velocity, Maya is interested in all the quiet she can get. “I’m working on being a little less busy,” she says. “Doing half as much twice as well is my current goal.” For now, she’s still able to wander museums and walk through New York without the celebrity disguise of sunglasses and a baseball hat. She’s soaking it in because she knows that come this winter, she’ll be under an arena-size spotlight thanks to Sunrise on the Reaping—and that for her, everything might be about to change.

I am the great joiner! On Stranger Things, I was joining a cast that had done the first two seasons. That held a different kind of weight. With this film, everyone had this newness to them. We are all in it together and will sink or swim as a team. There’s something relaxing about that.

Elle’s Effie is amazing. People are going to lose their minds. I was like, Whoa, that’s how you step on set! I had a different amount to work with. Amanda has three minutes total screen time as Wiress, and what we’ve seen of her character is someone who has been transformed by the events that unfold in Sunrise. We don’t know what she was like before that trauma happened.

I read the book a bunch of times. I rewatched the movies. I was deep in the script. Wiress was not a bright-eyed Capitol kid. She’s a tribute fresh off her win, which she accomplished in a really unusual way. She didn’t hurt anybody. Someone who wins the Games without betraying their moral compass is an extremely special person.

How much power corrupts. It’s not the person in power; it’s what power does to the person. That’s a very clear thesis that runs through the whole story. To make something that is both popular and anti-fascist is pretty crazy. What the books and films are doing is like protest art in a pretty explicit way.

I can get really nervous when I realize how much attention is on something like this. If I start looking at whether people are happy about the casting and the discourse that follows, it’s like the Eye of Sauron. I’m working on caring less about that.

1. Maya’s cowriting contributions are also featured on Phoebe Bridgers’s latest album, Lost Weekend.

The scene when she comes out of her coma—the whole crew got quiet; you could hear a pin drop. When nobody breathes, everyone knows that magic is happening. It grounded me to earth like a big tree. It woke me up. Like a little arrow saying, “Go that way.” And I’ve been following it ever since.

In the beginning of my career, when I was balancing fashion and acting, I had a boyfriend once call me an “AMW”—an actress, model, whatever. The “whatever” was so charged. I had some embarrassment around it. And I wanted to be like, “I’m not a model.” But I didn’t like “actress.”

When I go to Milan to work with Mrs. Prada and Raf Simons, I consistently feel good about myself. Even my husband pointed it out. I was recently auditioning for a project where the character needed to be really confident and he was like, “Be who you are when we go to Milan.”

I filmed Hunger Games and One Night Only back-to-back. They each have these two sorts of apocalyptic fascist governments. My joke on the set of Hunger Games was like, “Beetee,2 we have to have sex right now. We’ve got two hours—let’s go!” Government control in your bed? Horrible. The idea of taking it to that degree is just repellent. I want to be able to explore love on my terms. Bedtime TV, sex, kissing, and love. I mean, who would I be without these things? It would be punishment and torture.

2. Beetee Latier, played by Kelvin Harrison Jr., is an inventor and fellow former victor from District 3 who returns to the arena with Wiress for the Third Quarter Quell. Jeffrey Wright originally portrayed the character.

I consider myself a hopeless romantic. The closest we get to magic is what can happen between two people. Until I actually fell in love with my husband, I was falling in love all the time. I fall in love with my friends every day. I love them very romantically, like how can we spend Christmas together romantically and familially? My biggest heartbreaks of my life have been friendships ending.

It hits different because when you fall in love in a romantic way with someone, some part of you understands that there is a chance that it might expire. There are a lot of things that have to go right for it to work. But with a friendship, you don’t go in with that armor up. You go in so vulnerable, and when it doesn’t work, it’s the biggest tears I’ve ever shed. I hesitate to say this because it’s embarrassing, but I talk about my biggest friendship heartbreak from high school at least monthly, probably weekly. It was really formative.

I’m not going to lie and be like, “I never for one second considered it.” I genuinely thought about it. But the only thing I’ve known that I wanted to do on my wedding day since I was a kid was walk with the whole wedding party.

And they were, to their credit, extremely noninvasive. No one saw them. No one was upset by it. No one got hurt. I was kind of happy that those photographs ran in People because I had thought about doing a story about the dress. I worked with Prada, so it felt like a way to honor them without having to go out in public and say, “Look at me.”

I am not egomaniacal enough to think I will ever not be in my post–Stranger Things era. It will always be a beautiful shadow of my life. I will still consider myself to be in the aftermath of these experiences.

Exactly. I will sign posters until the day I die. Happily so.

#6951. All the Looks From the Inaugural Airbnb.Org Gala

From stars like Charli XCX, Serena Williams, Greta Lee, and Cynthia Erivo

From the Academy Museum Gala to the LACMA Art+Film Gala to the Baby2Baby Gala, there is no shortage of annual black-tie fundraising events and star-studded balls in Los Angeles. So when a new one pops up with a crowd that includes everyone from artists to athletes to actors to philanthropists, you know it’s a good one.

Last night, Airbnb hosted its first-ever gala, welcoming the likes of Zoe Saldaña, Serena Williams, and Matthew McConaughey to Milk Studios to celebrate a new funding model for its nonprofit sector, Airbnb.org. Founded in 2020, the sub-section was formed to provide free temporary housing to those in crisis, supporting people impacted by natural disasters, families traveling for medical care, individuals fleeing domestic violence, and more.

From yesterday onwards, the company known for its short-term rentals and unique global experiences is donating a portion of its revenue from every stay to its charity—with a goal of raising $500 million over the next five years, to be able to provide one million nights of free housing each year.

#6949. Whitney Peak Is Not Online But About to Be Everywhere

She doesn’t read the comments, doesn’t think of herself as a public figure, but still gets followed through airports by screaming teens. Now, as Lenore Dove Baird in The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping, she’s bracing for a fandom she can’t fully evade.

Whitney Peak was walking home late at night, headphones on, when she saw the fight. A group of people were arguing loudly across the street, and things were getting physical. She ducked in and out of a bodega and was steps from her front door when somebody grabbed her arm. It was one of the women who’d been shouting down the block, now yelling in Whitney’s face.

“You’re that girl from that movie! Can I get a photo?” Everyone crowded into a group selfie, recounts Whitney now, smiling into a cup of coffee. “And then they all went back across the street and continued the fight,” she says.

Whitney, now 23, first broke out in HBO Max’s 2021 Gossip Girl reboot with a lead role as scholarship student Zoya Lott. She went on to play the teenage Becca in Hocus Pocus 2 and has since starred alongside Sabrina Carpenter in the singer’s “Nonsense” video and started a partnership with Chanel. It’s a résumé that means sometimes people pause fistfights to speak to her, and sometimes packs of adolescents charge after her through airports.

“There was a group of 13-year-old girls, and they were adorable,” she says of another recent encounter. “I was walking ahead of them with my headphones on and they ran up behind me, so all I could feel was the stomping.”

In hindsight, it’s all been a preview of the fandom Whitney is about to meet with The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping. In the film, she plays Lenore Dove Baird, a fierce, musical girl whose great love is Haymitch Abernathy. (Fans know Haymitch as Katniss Everdeen’s weary mentor, but in this story, he’s a teenager who is separated from Lenore Dove when he’s forced into being a tribute for the 50th Hunger Games.) But despite all the excitement around the movie—and Whitney’s nearly half a million Instagram followers—she says she’s not super online, and she insists, “I don’t carry myself as a public figure. I never think about my career as a whole—I feel like I just got here.”

Even so, the fuss over this latest role will undoubtedly still shape her life in some way. Maybe that’s the strange bargain of playing characters people love: You can choose not to live online, but the work follows you into the world.

No, I met her for the first time two months ago on the street. We live in close neighborhoods. She was just walking by, and I was like, “Rachel??”

She was like, “This is crazy how we’re meeting.” I asked, “How about lunch or something?” but she was running back home. When the casting announcement came out, she sent me a DM and put out a really sweet message.1 Her saying that on the internet was big, because obviously people love her movie and The Hunger Games. It was very generous of her and speaks levels to her character.

1. Rachel tagged Whitney in a post where she wrote, “I know she’s gonna do the Baird name proud.”

Rachel and I were sending texts back and forth about that. I was like, “It never occurred to me that we looked alike.” But seeing the side-by-side pictures, I was like, “Wow. We really could be family.” It’s encouraging to me that I have her preparing me for the overwhelm that comes with joining such a franchise. She didn’t want it to be scary but wanted me to know it can get pretty intense. I get emotional because I hadn’t really let myself think about it, and then talking to her, it comes to the surface. I’m such a fan of hers. And this freaking cast is so insane.

We met in person for the first time at a chemistry read,2 and I walked in trying to be normal, and then Joe just came in like, “What’s up?” and put his phone and his cigarettes on the table, totally relaxed. It was immediately easy. The next time I saw him, we were shooting in Germany and he drove by our hotel in this black sprinter van, screaming my name out the window.

Our actual first scene together, we shot in this beautiful countryside field, and I remember being really, really nervous. But by the end, we were just playing. It made everything concrete between our characters and the two of us. Joe is such a goofball and really loves to laugh, which is one of my favorite traits in people. You can’t be self-serious when everybody is keeping it light. It’s kind of like, Tap in.

2. Whitney’s team has a running bit about her post-audition self-assessments: If she texts, “I bombed that,” they assume she got the job. The one time she felt an audition went really well, she didn’t get it. “I really don’t think it’s humility,” she says. “I have no fucking clue what went on in there.”

We did a lot of prep. I remember we would hang out pretty much every single day and just run the scenes over and over.

It’s one step at a time for me. I don’t really read reviews or comments or messages, and I’m not online very much. Obviously, you still care what people think.3 You want people to like your work. I feel like I live a pretty chill life4 considering my profession, but I do lean into it, and I can be freaked out and overwhelmed but still do it. And I’m going to tell you with this one, I was really freaked out.

3. Whitney also finds it difficult to watch herself perform. Once a project is finished, she says, “It’s no longer yours,” and she prefers to let it go. She’s making an exception for Sunrise on the Reaping though: “There’s so much of it that I didn’t see.” She is excited to watch but expects to be covering her eyes when her scenes come on.

4. Her approach to staying chill is refreshingly simple: “When I’m not working, I really prioritize being in my life and in my relationships.” Her non-Panem agenda includes reading and catching up with family and friends, two of whom are expecting a baby. Whitney is already halfway through knitting some tiny yellow clothes.

My PR team would probably be like, “No, shut up,” but I was! I don’t mean to be self-effacing or self-deprecating. I’m just being completely honest that I felt way in over my head with this. When something just feels so much larger than anything you could have conceived and it’s something people love, with a character who is really important to the story, and it’s just little old me doing all of that…I had to not think about it and just do it.

I let myself make a fool of myself. I had to sing in front of people, and I did it really badly just to get that out of the way and get a good laugh out of everybody. And then I felt like I could just kind of go. I was also listening to a lot of music: Irma Thomas, Bettye Swann, Kendra Morris.

I’m a huge music listener. But I wouldn’t say that I can actually play. I have a keyboard and a guitar that I fiddle with. I just kind of strum and see what sounds nice. I learned how to play the accordion...well, I learned how to play one song on the accordion.5 I’m a perfectionist, so I was really overdoing it. It’s all I wanted to do every single day.

5. There’s sweet irony in saying, “I can’t really play any instruments,” and then rattling off three you own and enjoy. But this is on-brand for Whitney, who is humble about her many talents.

Her relationship to the people she loves. We both value that a lot, and we both value community a lot. And there are so many ways I want to be more like her. She’s very outspoken, and she’s very, very self-possessed. There’s no second thought in her mind before she jumps into action. She trusts her gut, and she just cannot stand to watch anybody be in pain or be treated poorly. And she takes up space! I tend to retreat into myself. I’m just more of a thinker than a talker.

I’ll just say this: Bring a box of tissues.

#6947. The Anti-Capsule Wardrobe Theory: How to Build Outfits Around Statement Pieces

White T-shirt and jeans? Never heard of her.

Deep within the racks of my gloriously exploding closet, I have tiger-printed coats, expansive zebra jackets done in distinct babydoll silhouettes, feathered hats and boas, floor-length maxi dresses from the 1960s, and antique opera coats with shredded linings that have seen better days. I don’t, however, have the staples of what most people would probably associate with a classic capsule wardrobe. Basic white T-shirts, a normal pair of jeans (I like mine comically oversized by Vaquera, or studded in star- and heart-shaped metal piercings from Demna’s Balenciaga era), and a tailored black blazer don’t exist in my wardrobe. I’ve tried them, and they didn’t work for me. The same goes for accessories: A well-made black leather handbag will simply never bring me as much joy (and use!) as a crystal-covered cat minaudière. I realize I’m probably the weird one. But I’ve also never felt any particular urge to dress like everyone else.

One example is statement coats. Living in New York, more often than not, a good coat is your entire outfit and the only thing you see as the chill hits the air. I collect coats like some people collect coins or watches. I do own a handful of neutral wool coats, but I barely wear them. If it has an unusual texture, unique print, or shape that I know I’m comfortable wearing, it instantly becomes a part of my frequent rotation. Like anything else I repeat and wear regularly, I group pieces in my head into categories of recurring silhouettes or pairings that I know create the kind of impactful statement I like.

Here’s how my anti-capsule wardrobe pairings manifest: patterned, voluminous shorter coats (like the zebra one I’m wearing above) get paired with tighter silhouettes on the bottom: a skinny pant; printed, lace, or colored tights; or a miniskirt. So long as I have something as basic (in my world) as a pair of red tights, I know I can make a short, chunky coat work and that I’m going to love the way it looks. Longer duster-style jackets in quilted materials or various prints are another building block I like to use. For me, they add both a surprising element of old-world formality and a bohemian spirit to a more casual combination like a graphic T-shirt and linen pants. They also work with any dress of a similar length, and the more interesting the pattern clashes, the better. These are instant outfit-makers for me.

My pro-statement wardrobe is something I’ve built up over years of experimentation and analyzing some of my favorite style icons like Iris Apfel, Diana Vreeland, and Grace Jones. My eccentric pairings have been learned by trying many things that didn’t work. And they’ve been improved by looking at how some of the greats embraced various pieces. For example, another nontraditional standout building block that I like to use is big skirts with funky features. I think Miuccia Prada said it best: “For me, the skirt is like a T-shirt.” When I truly don’t know what to wear, I know that I can always grab a big skirt (many found vintage or secondhand): a cavernous black-and-white tartan option by Comme des Garçons, a retro-influenced sequin gem from Prada, or a dark feminine option from Simone Rocha, so long as it feels unique. I pair it with literally anything: T-shirt, scarf top, printed button-down.

Sometimes my formula for integrating eccentric pieces eschews silhouette and is more about the print. I’ve created certain rules: stripes look good with other stripes, especially if the stripes come in the form of a big coat. Animal prints like tiger and zebra are fun when clashed together if at least one of them comes in a neutral shade like black, white, or brown. Thinking in terms of vivid contrasts is the easiest way to plan outfits with statement pieces. Sporty neon heeled sneakers from Martine Rose will always be a favorite with a more formal-leaning floral maxi dress, in my opinion. I’m also not opposed to wearing black: I think some of the best anti-capsule building blocks, like a ruffled, tiered long tutu skirt, sometimes need the severity of black to ground them.

My anti-capsule theory also has a lot to do with accessories. Instead of white T-shirts, I have some really wild, over-the-top vintage statement necklaces that I gravitate to when I don’t know what else to wear. Some of them are so big that they deliberately cover a neckline I don’t like. I also think a great vintage bag, covered in beadwork or with a hint of whimsy, does all the talking when you need it to.

The point isn’t to own less or blend in; it’s to know exactly which wild pieces make you feel most like yourself. Finding special standouts that spark joy rather than those that exist solely to fit in with everything else makes getting dressed a powerful experience. The anti-capsule wardrobe theory is just that.

#6943. The 14 Best Leather Colognes That Are Perfect for the Cold Weather Ahead

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Just as versatile as your favorite leather jacket.

You pass a shoe store, a real one with old-school styles, and catch that scent of new leather. You slip on an old beat up leather jacket and get that deep, squeaky earthy hit. You slide into a leather banquette at your town’s most popular restaurant. Ever wanted that scent, in any variety, bottled up? That’s what a leather fragrance gives you. Warm, rich, and earthy, and often with a smoky edge, they’re especially great for fall and winter, though the right one can work year-round.

Even vegetarians like myself can get behind a sexy leather cologne; they’re not distilling real animal leather, if you were wondering. Since leather has no extractable essential oil that can be used for perfume, perfumers recreate its scent using synthetic or botanical accords, often blended with smoky, resinous notes like birch tar, cade, and labdanum. It’s creating the idea of leather. The result can range from rugged and intense to soft and smooth like suede. There are plenty of different takes on leather fragrance out there, but these are our favorites.

This fragrance from Cremo mostly features white moss and amber, but the base notes of smooth suede come through at the end to create a warm, elevated aroma. Don’t be fooled by the low price point—you’ll still get a sophisticated scent of suede, though it’s less aggressive than its spicier and woodier alternatives.

This Vintage cologne from John Varvatos is about as manly as it gets. Its warm, woody overall scent with hints of patchouli and tobacco leaf underneath leave you with a long-lasting air of sophistication and refinement. The warm spice notes perfectly complement crisp autumn air while the patchouli and suede offer warmth during the winter like a fireplace in a cozy cabin.

There’s no mistaking the leather in this cologne—it permeates the entire package—but instead of being aggressive, it comes across as sophisticated. It’s a gentlemanly scent that’s surprisingly wearable for both day and night, thanks to the amber and patchouli at the base that give it a warm, herbal vibe. Think of it as less “cowboy” and more “oil baron.”

If Ombré Leather is Tom Ford’s easy, toned-down version of leather, Tuscan Leather is the aggressive, literal version. The leather almost overpowers everything else—almost—but the sweeter top notes allow for a delicious opening that adds smoothness to the leather and the warm, woody dry down lends a layered elegance. The leather here is top quality. Not new car smell, but perfectly worn-in bespoke shoes or the seats of a vintage Ferrari.

At first spray, the fruity plum and peach top notes take over, but pretty soon they give way to a deeper, more layered fragrance that really does call to mind the stacks of an old library. The sweetness doesn’t entirely go away, but as the florals and leather appear, like pages turning in a book, more depth is revealed.

Designed as an enhancer—a lighter fragrance to layer with others to change the vibe—this interpretation of leather smells almost exactly like the real thing. It’s slick, smooth, and warm, with a sharp edge that’s masculine in a ’70s Camaro kind of way. Sure, you can wear it as it was meant (that is, with another cologne), but we say try it by itself to really drive that leather home.

Tru Western’s Leather Vintage Label cologne collection is a great place to start when it comes to leather fragrances. Each of the five scents highlights a different aspect of leather, but No. 1 brings out woody notes—and a bit of fresh clementine—to give the leather accord a classic, straightforward backdrop that lets the leather accord be the star player. For something a bit richer with amber notes, try No. 4 (Deep Leather).

We’re big fans of Ranger Station, the Nashville-based niche fragrance brand known for its nature-inspired scents—and it all started with the Leather + Pine candle. After the candle took off, Ranger Station of course had to turn the scent into a wearable fragrance. Slightly different from the candle’s scent, the EDP is a blend of tanned leather, pine sap, amber, and sandalwood, which creates an alluring blend of sweet and savory.

We can’t talk about leather colognes without mentioning Malin + Goetz’s famous take on the scent. Simply called “leather,” it’s a rugged blend of florals, spices, and woods that emphasizes pepper and lily of the valley along with leather accord. And while it features a handful of floral notes, it never leans into powdery territory; it remains a solidly masculine scent, though it suits pretty much everyone.

For a bit of a more intense, spicy take on leather, Desert Nights is the cologne to go with. It’s inspired by Marrakech after sunset, layering luxurious oud and sandalwood with earthy patchouli and moss, and the spices up top carry through. It’s seductive and perfect for a date night or winter outing.

Arquiste is known for complex fragrances inspired by historical events and specific landscapes, and Nocturnality is one of our favorites—we even included it in our 2025 Cologne Awards. The inspiration for this one comes from mid-’90s New York City nightlife; picture an underground club with humid air, booze, cigarette smoke, and lots of people dancing. In a less abstract sense, that means latex, leather, and oud, which combine for a dark, sexy scent.

Most leather colognes lean on warmth, sweetness, and spice to complement the leather notes (which is partly why you often see a shift toward leather colognes in the winter), but this one does things a bit differently. Yes, there’s spice in the opening, but there’s also citrus that moves on to florals in the middle before drying down to earthy, musky notes. The leather is apparent all the way through, but it feels lighter and fresher than many others on this list.

There’s something very nostalgic about this fragrance but it doesn’t smell old-fashioned. Instead, it calls to mind dark rooms, sexy rendezvous, sweaty nights, and skin tight jeans. It’s smoky and spicy, but layered with musk and pungent black leather for an über masculine vibe that’s impossible to ignore. When you wear it, expect to be noticed—and for the compliments to come nonstop.

Perfumehead’s scents, like all the best fragrances, tell stories and paint pictures. This boozy, smoky juice is inspired by the cowboy-filled honky-tonks of the 1970s Sunset Strip, and one whiff is more than enough to take you there. The final effect of the fragrance isn’t especially Western or aggressive, but more cozy and sensual, like finding a vintage leather jacket that miraculously fits you like a glove.

#6942. The Best Volufiline Products for Subtly Plumper-Looking Skin

Touted as a filler alternative, dermatologists say the effects of Volufiline are much more subtle.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As conversations around the beauty side effects of GL-P1s spread, and on the heels of the PDRN craze, a new player has entered the skin-plumping game with big promises. Volufiline, a patented cosmetic ingredient first developed by the French company Sederma, is an oil-based blend of hydrogenated polyisobutene (a synthetic hydrocarbon polymer) and an extract derived from the root of Anemarrhena asphodeloides, also known as Zhi Mu, a plant traditionally used in Asian medicine. Deborah R. Spey, MD, a board-certified dermatologist at Schweiger Dermatology in Livingston, New Jersey, says the ingredient has gained attention for its purported ability to subtly plump the skin and improve the appearance of volume loss, and for its claim to be a topical replacement for injectable filler.

According to Spey, the active component in Volufiline is believed to encourage existing fat cells to store more lipids and support the maturation of new fat cells, potentially increasing volume in the deeper layers of the skin. “However, there is very limited scientific data supporting its effectiveness, and any reported plumping effects have been quite modest,” she tells ELLE. “While the concept is promising, more research is needed to determine how effectively a topical formulation can deliver these results.”

Beverly Hills esthetician, Candace Marino adds that the ingredient is thought to encourage the fat cells just under the skin to store more lipids, which gives the area a softer, fuller look. “Think subtle improvement over time, not overnight change,” says Marino.

Volufiline has a completely different composition and mechanism of action compared to injectable dermal fillers. Marino says they’re not comparable. “Filler is injected, immediate, and can actually lift and reshape. Volufiline is a gentle topical option for someone who isn’t ready for injectables or wants to maintain between appointments.”

Spey further explains how the ingredient works compared to traditional filler. “Many traditional dermal fillers are made from cross-linked hyaluronic acid and are injected into specific tissue planes to restore lost volume and provide structural support,” Spey explains. Volufiline, on the other hand, is a topical ingredient intended to encourage the skin’s own plumping process. “There is a significant difference between injectable fillers, which can reach deeper structural layers, and a topical product, which has limitations in how deeply it can penetrate the skin. Volufiline should not be considered a replacement for injectable dermal fillers, particularly for individuals seeking noticeable volume restoration,” she clarifies.

Spey says that an ingredient like Volufiline may be a reasonable option for individuals looking for subtle plumping or hoping to improve the appearance of mild volume loss. However, she cautions that because it is oil-based, it may not be ideal for those with oily or acne-prone skin, as it could potentially aggravate these conditions. She says it also has not been adequately studied for use during pregnancy, so pregnant individuals should avoid using it unless advised otherwise by their health care provider.

Marino recommends the ingredient for anyone noticing early volume loss, hollowness under the eyes, flattening in the cheeks, or thinner lips. Drier and more mature skin tends to do especially well with it. If you’re acne-prone, pay attention to the oils in the formula.

Volufiline can be paired with other skin care ingredients, including peptides, ceramides, squalane, hyaluronic acid, niacinamide, and growth factors. However, Spey recommends avoiding layering it directly with retinoids, such as retinol, or strong exfoliating acids to minimize the potential for irritation. “If you use retinol regularly, you can incorporate Volufiline into your morning routine and reserve retinol for the evening, or alternate their use,” she suggests. In serum form, apply it to clean skin and follow it with a moisturizer. Cream formulations can serve as the moisturizing step in your routine.

There is a benefit to combining Volufiline with a retinol regimen (though not at the same time). According to Marino, retinol works on texture and collagen, and Volufiline works on fullness. “If your skin is sensitive, use retinol at night and Volufiline in the morning. Or, depending on the formula, you can layer them in the evening,” she suggests.

This highly concentrated serum is meant to be used as a targets treatment, not all over the face. Apply it under moisturizer to areas that need a volume boost like under eye, cheeks, and temples.

Seoul Ceuticals combined two buzzy ingredients, Volufiline and snail mucin into one serum alongside an extra moisturizing dose of hyaluronic acid and calming cica. The results, according to users is that it absorbs easily and layers well under other skin care, and results are visible rather quickly.

The under eye area is one that can reap the most benefits from Volufiline. This formula combines plumping Volufiline with peptides, collagen, and snail mucin to smooth fine lines, tighten saggy skin, and give hollowed under eyes a more plumped effect. There’s also some caffeine and azulene to brighten up any dark circles or discoloration.

Taking the ingredient benefits beyond the face, B.CLINICX formulated this body cream specifically to help with volume loss and elasticity in the breast area. The cream has Volufiline and a low molecular weight of collagen to absorb deeper into skin to help rebuild the structure for a more lifted appearance.

This rich cream formula has hefty doses of both Volufiline and collagen to help sallow areas of skin look fuller and more bouncy. For added plumpness, glycerin, hyaluronic acid, and a blend of oils do the moisturizing magic to attract and replenish moisture in skin.

While consistency is key, this cream-in-serum treatment promises visible results in a week. The serum also combines Volufiline with collagen to tackle the plumping and firming, while amino acids and hyaluronic acid strengthen the skin barrier.

What makes the revival feel fresh rather than nostalgic is the execution. Today’s versions trade the stiff, boxy bulk of decades past for fluid tailoring, richer fabrics, and proportions that feel intentional rather than costumey. Wool-cashmere blends drape with weight and movement, while cocooned, oversized cuts sit seamlessly alongside razor-sharp details. Whatever the case, the structured silhouette reads as confidence.

But aside from that, the beauty of the power shoulder is its efficiency. Throw one over jeans and a white tee and the outfit looks styled. Layer one over a sleek suit and you’ve got an instant office classic. It’s the rare piece that does the heavy lifting, so everything underneath can stay simple.

Sharp padded shoulders and sleek, belted tailoring come together in this essential trench coat. The cinched waist creates a dramatic hourglass shape, while the classic cut keeps it versatile enough to wear every day.
