#6930. Fall 2026’s Biggest Pedicure Trends Are Here—Shop the Shades

From dark reds and chocolate browns to milky pinks and French tips.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Fall has officially arrived at our fingertips. First, Dakota Johnson’s “brown sugar espresso” nails brought a warm, creamy brown into the conversation; then Anne Hathaway’s “cherry noir” manicure made a convincing case for the season’s deep, glossy reds. If the autumnal manicures so far are any indication, this season is shaping up to be deliciously moody polish-wise. But what about your toes?

While they may be spending more time tucked inside boots and sneakers than on display in your go-to summer sandals, there’s no need to abandon them. A fresh coat of polish may be hidden from everyone else, but “nothing is going to feel better curled up next to the fireplace or snuggled inside your slippers than freshly painted toes,” celebrity manicurist Stephanie Stone says on behalf of ORLY.

The color trends are particularly good this season. Thea Green MBE, founder of Nails.INC, looks to the richer, more textured feel of fall wardrobes for inspiration, pointing to “deep burgundies, chocolate browns, sophisticated grays, and jewel tones.” However, she also loves the contrast of a barely-there, milky pedicure against heavier knits, leather, and suede. Plus, you should prepare to keep your nails nice and short this season. As Stone notes, “you’re going to want to keep your toenails short and naturally shaped so they don’t keep bumping up against your shoes.”

So while your favorite fall manicures might be getting all the attention, don’t overlook what’s happening below. Ahead, experts share the fall 2026 pedicure trends predicted to take over—and the shades you can shop to try them yourself.

“The color of the moment is a deep vampy red,” says Stone. Green concurs, sharing, “Deep, delicious, almost-black reds are going to be one of the most sophisticated pedicure looks for fall 2026.” The shade is perfect for fall because it’s dark, moody, fashion-forward, and timeless. “I particularly love a rich burgundy on short, softly squared toenails with a beautiful high-gloss finish,” says Green.

“Chocolate brown is absolutely having its moment,” says Green. From deep espressos to lighter caramels, “we’re moving towards beautiful, edible-looking tones.” A chocolate pedicure feels warmer and softer than black, but has the same sophisticated impact.

Not every Fall pedicure needs to be dark. In fact, Green loves a delicate, barely-there pink against chunky knits and dark denim. “Soft, milky pinks are subtle, elegant, and give that lovely ‘your nails but better’ effect,” she says. The key is choosing a shade that looks softly polished rather than overly opaque, with a beautiful, glossy finish. “I love it on shorter, naturally rounded toenails for that perfectly groomed, effortless finish.”

“French tips are always in style, but can make you feel extra chic when worn on your toes in the fall,” says Stone. Since wearing the design on your feet can feel like such a statement, autumn is the perfect time to try it since your pedicure is more for you than anyone else.

“We’re seeing a move towards finishes that are a little more interesting than a traditional crème,” says Green. “Whether that’s chrome, pearl, or iridescent is completely up to you.” Stone makes a strong case for cat eye. “Cat eye nail art is still all the rage, and it makes for a great pedicure design too.” Green adds, “ I absolutely love the idea of a delicate iridescent pedicure for fall. The effect should be almost translucent and beautifully luminous, catching the light as you move.” Any fun finish makes a pedicure feel special without relying on elaborate nail art.

”One of my favorite emerging color directions is what I call the new neutrals: mushroom, greige, taupe and soft, earthy grey,” says Green. These shades are perfect for a minimalist, incredibly chic look. These warmer grey tones make them much more wearable than traditional cold grey shades, particularly for fall. “I love this look with short, neatly shaped toenails and a glossy finish.”

Of course, it’s possible that these casts and boxes have nothing to do with The Batman: Part II, or—if you feel like being a conspiracy theorist—that they were purposefully left in the background to get the rumor mill going. Either way, I’m invested.

#6927. This $50 Amazon Find Made My Dark, Dated Countertops Look Like Expensive Marble

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Growing up with a mom who has a knack for home improvement (and a love for HGTV) has certainly rubbed off on me. I’m notorious for finding furniture and decor on the street and giving them a second life. DIY projects have also become a hobby of mine, with one of my recent favorites being the marble contact paper I installed in my kitchen. The dark granite countertops in my rental made my space feel so, well, dark.

I knew the process would likely involve plenty of trial and error, so I ordered two rolls from Amazon (you can buy the exact ones I used below). These come in a range of sizes, from 11.8” x 78.7” to 47.2” x 196”—pull out a measuring tape and choose the best option for your space.

I’ll be honest: The project was anything but easy, though that was partly because I tackled it solo. Yes, having a second set of hands would have made the process much smoother, as peeling off the backing and applying the contact paper can get tricky.

Whether you’re taking on the project alone or with a helping hand, here’s step one: Clean your countertops so the paper goes on smoothly. I’d also suggest buying this smoothing tool kit from Amazon, as it was a real game-changer for me. When you’re ready to get started, peel back a small section of the backing and carefully press the adhesive side onto the counter. I worked little by little, using the smoothing tool to eliminate any air bubbles as I went. There are also grid lines on the backing, making it easy to measure and trim the paper to fit your countertops.

My countertops before I installed the contact paper.

From start to finish, the project took me roughly seven hours on a Saturday. Again, part of that was because I was working alone, not to mention it was my first time using contact paper—and those pesky air bubbles were my biggest challenge. My countertops also have several curved edges, which only made the process harder.

Mine are far from perfect, but I don’t regret installing them in the slightest. Not only have they brightened up my kitchen tenfold, but they also haven’t budged in the year or so I’ve had them. If marble doesn’t align with your style, there are a slew of other iterations on Amazon, from wood finishes to stark white. The beauty of this contact paper lies in its versatility—it can also double as wallpaper or be used to spruce up a nightstand or dresser. I’ll leave the decision up to you.

#6925. How to Style Flannel Shirts Like the Cool Girls

Muted plaids, sleek layers, and smart contrasts make flannel feel instantly elevated.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Once associated solely with the lumberjack aesthetic, flannel shirts have become a wardrobe staple. Flannel is, at its core, a remarkably versatile fabric—soft, structured, and warm without bulk, with a texture that looks expensive when styled with intention. It’s timeless, perfect for in-between weather, and, you have to admit, effortlessly cool.

The trick to elevating it lies in contrast. Pair a brushed-cotton shirt with sharp tailoring and it instantly looks considered. These days, flannel is being worn with everything from denim to satin skirts, and each pairing adds up to an elevated outfit formula.

Beyond the pairings, the details do the heavy lifting. Choose muted, earthy plaids over loud ones, and let the shirt’s softness play against something sleek, like pleated trousers or an oversized fit thrown over a slip dress. Layer it under a cozy coat or knot it at the waist, and flannel stops feeling like a seasonal afterthought. It becomes the piece the whole outfit is built around.

Ahead, we’ve rounded up elevated flannel outfit ideas to inspire you, built around fall’s most essential piece.

The unexpected pairing of black cargo shorts, a blue check flannel, and black loafers works surprisingly well. It reads as casual with a side of cool-girl attitude.

Pairing a practical flannel shirt with a romantic pink satin skirt and black flip-flops brings a fresh approach to fall styling. Trade the flip-flops for ankle boots and add a cropped jacket when temperatures drop.

We love how this outfit pairs an everyday plaid top with wrap-skirt trousers and statement yellow sneakers. The pop of color keeps the look playful, while the easy plaid grounds it in classic fall style.

Styled with wide-leg jeans, this look reads casual, but it’s the luxe accessories that instantly elevate it. A quilted Chanel bag, sleek loafers, and oversized sunglasses add polish without losing the easy feel of the denim.

Tied around the waist, this flannel shirt pairs seamlessly with a checked skirt, even with their different prints. Worn with a chunky knit and shearling suede jacket, every layer and detail feels intentional.

#6924. It’s Time to Buy an Art TV. These Are the 4 Best Options.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These are slim, glare-free, and give your house an extra touch of class.

Whether you call it an art TV, a frame TV, or a gallery TV, we’re all talking about the same thing. An anti-reflective matte screen, paired with a thin (often wood or wood-looking) bezel so that when it’s turned off you can approximate a gallery painting or photographs instead of staring at a blank black screen. These trendy TVs are the new best way for home design lovers to not compromise their aesthetic visions while still entertaining. Art TVs are for the people who care about their home design as much as their actual TV watching experience.

The breakout, genre-defining example was The Frame. Samsung’s 2017 release was the first TV model that truly masqueraded as an art piece on your wall. Instead of a big blank space on your wall, you now have art when you're not watching TV, and a TV when you are. Got a date coming over? Switch that black screen to one of any classic artworks from The Met or one of many museums worldwide. It took a few iterations to get right, but less than a decade later Samsung was doing enough right that other TV manufacturers, Hisense, LG, and TCL are following suit.

These four art TVs are a marvelous blend of form and function. I tried to pick a range of sizes between 50 and 75 inches, but most of these TVs come in every size between 55 inches and 85 inches. Whatever your budget or space allows, you’ll find the right one here.

Samsung still makes the best art TV out there. With the introduction of the Frame Pro line, Samsung made the first premium art TV. Premium picture quality, premium sound (for once), and—if you’re willing to pay a handsome $4.99 a month—the largest selection of fine art you can get on one of these. Even for the low price, it’s annoying to essential pay the Met a subscription fee to use one of the main features of your TV. But, here’s the upside. It’s all real art and no AI; refreshing in this economy. The free library cycles through about 30 new pieces of art a month, so even if you aren’t paying you’re getting a new influx of (real) art periodically.

Free with your package is Samsung's One Connect Box. This is the real game changer when it comes to aesthetics. It opens you up to put your Frame anywhere the TV fits on your wall, while your cable box and gaming consoles are plugged into the OCB and stowed out of sight. The box and TV communicate wirelessly, so you don’t have to worry about stowing cables out of sight (or in the wall) to get the true Frame experience of passing your TV off as art when your unsuspecting neighbor stops by.

Compared to the more ubiquitous Google TV experience you’ll get on the Hisense and TCL, Samsung’s user interface takes some getting used to if you haven’t driven one of these TVs before. Even with familiarity, it’s still not my favorite to navigate.

The Frame Pro might be the flagship, but Samsung is still updating its regular Frame model each year with great success. This 2026 update to the original Frame is a slightly more affordable, slightly less impressive version of what you get with the Frame Pro. It’s equipped with everything Samsung has figured out over the years: an anti-reflective matte display, options for customizable bezels, and a massive accumulated gallery of artwork to choose from.

It may not technically reach the heights of the Pro, but the picture quality is super clear and the colors are vivd enough for most viewers to be satisfied. On the whole, it has a lot of the same pros (and all the same cons) as the beefier Pro for a few hundred dollars cheaper.

If you need something smaller than 55-inches, this is the only art TV that clears the bar. Luckily, it is a very good one. The 2026 Hisense Canvas looks and sounds fantastic, the setup is way more streamlined than The Frame, and it runs Google TV, which has become my OS of choice due to the consistent performance and easy setup. In my team’s tests, we found this to be the best looking art TV for the money, even if it technically fell short of the Frame Pro.

The best part of the Hisense art TV experience is that you don't have to pay for the art. Not once, not ever. Plus, the CanvasTV even offers a 144 Hz refresh rate mode for gaming with low latency. Not that I’d recommend gaming on an art TV, but if it’s your only option, Hisense makes it at least tolerable. We only wish we had the convenience of the Samsung OCB with the free art offerings of the CanvasTV. Now, that would be heaven.

Dollars to donuts, this 75-inch TCL art TV is the best value in the category, costing roughly as much as a 55-inch Samsung Frame Pro. The budget-friendly display comes at the cost of performance and color. It's got muddier blacks that can make night scenes hard to watch and it's not as vibrant as The Frame or the CanvasTV.

For one, this TV does have one advantage that helps it outweigh the others; it's the only frame TV that comes close to being as thin as an actual piece of artwork. It only has one bezel option, but at least it’s a relatively neutral off-white.

The art selection is decent, and we like how easy the TV and Google OS makes it to share your own photos instead. TCL also boasts about the hundreds of thousands of pieces of AI artwork you can access and create. It solves the lack of content problem, but a computer plagiarizing Van Gogh is never going to be a fraction as beautiful as a picture of an actual Van Gogh.

In short, art TVs are meant to be able to double as framed artwork when they are not in use. The features of these TVs include:

For me, it’s got to be 65-inches. Even in my (relatively) small Brooklyn apartment, this is the sweet spot for TV size. Anything larger is really just for shits and giggles at that point. 55 inches is generally the smallest (aside from Hisense with its 50 inch) size of art TV you can get and it’s largely pretty acceptable, but know if you have a large wall that even that may feel small.

The OS is basically the whole look and feel of a smart TV interface. Roku City is comforting, but you won't get that here. The only options are Google TV or Samusng's Tizen OS. Between the Tizen interface and Google TV, we give the slight edge to the Google UI and OS. It's smoother and gives more options for integrating Google Photos—a great feature to have on an art TV. As far as TV operating systems go, though, they are pretty similar in terms of layout and amount of space dedicated to advertising.

We've done the hard work for you by spending years watching these TVs up close. We've seen them in action on showroom floors, in private demo sessions, and (most important of all) in our homes. We've awarded the best of the best. Needless to say, we did all the heavy lifting for you. Sometimes literally.

The actors unpack the morning-after fallout.

For three seasons, The Gilded Age fans have waited for Larry and Marian to get together. After their engagement went south, it seemed like it might never happen. Bu›t the morning after, it’s clear that Marian is devastated by what’s happened between the two of them, and Larry is left reeling as to what he did wrong.

Louisa: It's this huge transgression. Premarital sex was a cardinal sin, and it's something that would completely ruin a woman's reputation in society, completely leave her destitute, leave her without marriage options. She would be thought of as impure. We don't live in this context anymore.

It's really hard to deal with the fact that what might have been the best night of her life is something that is so bad because she's internalized that history of shame. He's not going to feel those consequences.

Louisa: Her father lied to her when he died about her situation financially, and he wasn't a very good father. Her mother died very young. And then there's Tom Raikes from season 1, and then the safe, arranged thing with Dashiell didn't work out, then there’s this mistrust with Larry because of the brothel situation. She's just been burned so many times in such an intense way that there's already a lot of trepidation. And there's a lot of pressure on her from society because she's had two failed engagements. The stakes are really, really high right now.

Harry: It takes the whole season. He's really struggling at trying to understand the gravity of what's happened, but he gets a full force realization in the morning after. Louisa was given one of the hardest tasks ever to shift from the most exquisite night of, finally, this couple we've been rooting for to be able to express their love getting together, and then in one cut we go to what is my favorite shot of the whole season, this POV of her in the mirror. My heart broke for her because you see instantly in one look, "Oh, her life is on the line.”

Beyond all of his desires to connect with her, Larry adores her and wants to be with her. He sees the person he loves the most in utter agony. He’s instantly confronting: My immaturity has now put the person who I care most about in a terrible situation.

Louisa: I was trying to figure out, what's she experiencing physically? Not to be TMI, but you know, stuff happens to your body after you have sex for the first time that can be uncomfortable physically, and if you don't know what's going on, you really suffer.

Harry: I feel like life is a continuous expansion of will-they-won’t-they. Am I gonna am I gonna brave up or am I gonna freak out again? We're all trying to expand our heart. We're also going through all this fear and insecurity. It's amazing to play because we get to confront the parts of ourselves that are closed or scared. This show is so beautiful because even though there's so many complications for every character, as it gets more complex, it gets deeper.

#6919. A Crash Course in Corduroy

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It’s the perfect time of year for a tough, warm fabric with tons of texture. Here’s how to work it into your wardrobe.

According to a recent and deeply unscientific poll conducted by yours truly, some guys are confounded by corduroy. They like it. They want to wear it. But they’re not quite sure how it figures into their wardrobes. This is a pity, because corduroy is great. It’s warm, it’s hard-wearing, it delivers texture galore, and it can work beautifully in the context of all different types of personal style.

So in the interest of getting these guys—and you!—properly outfitted, I’m offering this crash course in corduroy. It’ll give you the key information you need in order to understand the fabric’s history and how you can wear it now. Let’s dive in.

There’s a great story about the name corduroy being derived from the French phrase “corde du roi,” or “cord of the king.” It conjures up all sorts of noble associations and sounds cool, like so much menswear lore does. The problem is, it’s likely apocryphal. No one really knows the root of the word for sure. (But if you want to bust out that tidbit at a dinner party and let your friends believe it, we wouldn’t blame you.)

What we do know is that corduroy traces its roots back to a category of fabric called fustian—basically heavy cloth, often used for menswear, that was usually woven from cotton (though there have been woolen and linen variations over the many centuries that fustian has existed). The name might come from the city of Fustat, near Cairo, which manufactured that sort of cloth. Or maybe it doesn’t. More menswear lore!

What sets corduroy apart from other fustian cloths like velveteen and moleskin is its distinctive ribbing. That innovation is newer; it was developed in Manchester around the middle of the 18th century, when the city became a center of production for cotton fabrics like velvet and the modern iteration of corduroy. The ribs are known as wales, and they’re created by weaving extra thread into the base fabric and then trimming them to created tufted, vertical ridges offset by channels between them.

This “modern” corduroy quickly became a workwear staple thanks to its durability and warmth. It was only later that it migrated to British country clothing and, eventually, to the rest of the globe. It slowly filtered into U.S. academia throughout the first half of the 20th century, eventually leading to the well-known stereotype of the college professor with the well-worn and elbow-patched corduroy jacket. It hit the counterculture in the ’60s. And it absolutely boomed in the ’70s, showing up on the era’s leading men both on and off the silver screen.

Since then, as with all menswear staples, its popularity has ebbed and flowed. But like denim, it never truly goes out of style. It’s all about how you wear it.

The width of the wales is measured by how many you can fit into an inch, so an 8-wale corduroy has thicker ridges than, say, a 12-wale corduroy. Most “standard” corduroy sits around 11 to 13 wales per inch. Chunkier stuff has a lower number, while “pinwale” or “needlecord” corduroy is higher, around 16 or above. As you might guess, the chunkier stuff reads more rugged and the finer stuff feels more refined.

Developed in and named after the town of New Bedford, Massachusetts, this fabric has distinctive vertical ridges, loads of durability, and roots in the world of workwear. It’s not quite corduroy, though. The ridges are created in the way the base fabric is woven, not by adding additional threads and trimming them. Still, it’s worth having on your radar if you like the look and feel of corduroy.

Just … wear it. I know that sounds like fake advice, but it’s really not worth overthinking it. Yes, you’ll want to think about the weather, because corduroy can wear warmer than other fabrics. And you might want to get yourself used to the idea of the occasional whoosh of the ribbed fabric rubbing against itself. (It’s not as distracting as the crinkle of a nylon shell suit, but it’s audible.) But corduroy is in its current form because it works. It wears in beautifully, fends off the cold, and adds some always welcome depth and texture to any outfit.

If you’re still nervous, start with something simple, like a corduroy jacket—your call on whether it’s a sport coat or something more utilitarian—or some five-pocket cord pants. You could rock it as an overshirt (go wide wale) or as a button-up. You could even channel everyone from Wes Anderson to Robert Redford with a full corduroy suit, though you might want to avoid the elbow patches if you don’t want to look too professorial.

As for what to wear it with? Well, nothing too techy. A super-stretchy golf shirt doesn’t exactly match the vibe; ditto for polyester joggers. And nothing too fine or fancy, either. Because of its more casual and countryish vibe, corduroy might not be the best complement to spit-polished oxford shoes or a particularly delicate worsted wool. The idea is to look for a sense of cohesion with your outfit, and anything that reads as deliberately futuristic or self-consciously dressy could detract from that.

Otherwise, options abound, especially when you start talking about other classic menswear fabrics and items. Corduroy looks great with beefy oxford-cloth button-downs. It plays well with denim and chino and flannel and hopsack. And don’t be afraid of going all in on texture. Bust out a shaggy Shetland or cable-knit sweater. Bring some tweed into the mix. Corduroy shouldn’t be confusing; it should be easy. So play around a little. You’re bound to have a (sorry) wale of a good time.

#6916. How to Pull Off Coffee Nails, From Creamy Latte Shades to Deep Espresso

The cozy brown manicure trend works as a glossy neutral, a velvet cat-eye, or a caramel French tip.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There’s a lot to love about coffee, and even if you’re not a fan of the drink, the color itself, with a rich, deep brown, serves as the perfect neutral to pair with your favorite light or dark shade. On nails, it works seamlessly across skin tones. And don’t think that coffee brown is only reserved for cooler winter months. Nail experts share how to wear the shade as a base, accent, or main character color this fall and beyond.

Max Estrada, a professional nail artist and founder and creative director of eNailCouture based in Tucson, Arizona, calls the coffee shade “latte lacquer” to describe its range in the rich brown color family. “It’s essentially the beauty equivalent of walking into a gorgeous Italian café and being presented with an entire menu of indulgent coffee creations,” he says. “Think creamy cappuccino, warm caramel, rich mocha, toasted hazelnut, and deep espresso.” It’s a beautiful spectrum of warm, sophisticated neutrals inspired by different shades and textures of coffee.

Estrada notes that the trend ties into the gourmand fragrance movement. “We’ve fallen in love with perfumes that smell like vanilla, caramel, chocolate, and coffee, and now we’re bringing that same delicious, comforting luxury to our fingertips,” Estrada shares. According to Estrada, what separates coffee from other brown polish shades is its depth and richness.

When choosing your version of a coffee manicure, Viktoriia Voloshyna, international judge, award-winning nail educator, and founder of Tory Pro Nails Studio in Beverly Hills, suggests thinking of a coffee menu, from light to dark:Milk foam: a soft ivory with a gentle glowFlat white: a warm, creamy beigeLatte: a soft satin beigeCaramel: a rich, warm brownEspresso: a deep coffee brown or a dark chocolate brown

“All these shades mix beautifully on one hand. You can go from light to dark, like a gradient from milk to espresso, or do milky nails with one espresso accent nail,” says Voloshyna.

If very light shades aren’t your norm, Estrada recommends starting with a warm caramel latte, meaning creamy beige, toasted almond, or buttery caramel shades. For something more dramatic, he suggests rich mocha, dark chocolate, and espresso tones, particularly those with warm undertones that complement the natural complexion of the hands. “Personally, I think the secret is choosing colors with beautiful depth rather than flat, lifeless browns,” says Estrada.

To create the depth and dimension Estrada and Voloshyna refer to, mix a few coffee shades into a French manicure, a blended ombré, or a magnetic cat-eye finish. “One technique I particularly love is creating a beautiful caramel or chocolate French manicure and then layering a sheer milky nude over the entire design,” says Estrada. “It softens the contrast and creates this gorgeous creamy, diffused effect, almost like watching milk swirl into a freshly poured espresso.”

Although the darkness of coffee lacquer feels more seasonally appropriate in fall and winter, Voloshyna says it’s easily adaptable in spring and summer, too. “In spring and summer, I would go lighter and more sheer, like an iced latte: milky, translucent shades with a glossy finish. In fall and winter, it’s all about deeper mocha and espresso, and you can add cat-eye or a little shimmer for the holidays,” says Voloshyna.

For the lighter looks, Estrada also recommends a delicate caramel French tip or blended ombré finishes that combine the lighter latte shades.

In winter, Estrada goes more extravagant with deep mocha shades accented with gold chrome, espresso with a velvet magnetic finish, or rich chocolate with delicate metallic embellishments. “It’s the same beautiful color family, but with an entirely different personality depending on the season,” he adds.

The range of a coffee manicure works on different nail shapes, too. “A beautifully tapered, elongated almond nail in a rich mocha shade is incredibly sophisticated and almost sensual. It reminds me of the elegant, exaggerated fashion silhouettes of the 1990s. For something more architectural, I love a dramatic, boxy square nail with a caramel French manicure. It’s wonderfully nostalgic and fashionable,” explains Estrada.

Shorter nails can certainly wear the trend too, especially in a single warm cappuccino shade—but Estrada says longer extensions leave room to experiment with more elaborate gradients, sculpted details, and multidimensional finishes.

If you do go dark on longer nails, though, Voloshyna says to keep the nails thin, especially if you’re painting over a gel or acrylic base. “Coffee shades, especially the dark ones, make thick nails look heavy,” she notes.

The finish you choose changes everything, so choose wisely. “A beautiful high-gloss caramel manicure resembles liquid toffee or freshly poured espresso. It's rich, polished, and incredibly luxurious. But a matte finish creates an entirely different personality. Deep chocolate and mocha shades suddenly resemble suede, velvet, or the finest cashmere,” describes Estrada. Feel free to combine textures, too. Try a matte espresso nail with an ultra-glossy French tip or a chocolate magnetic cat-eye to create the look of crushed velvet.

Another technique Estrada uses to create dimension is layering a sheer milky color over glitter. Begin with a champagne, gold, or caramel glitter, then apply a translucent milky nude over the top. “Instead of having that very sharp, obvious sparkle, the glitter becomes beautifully diffused beneath the creamy layer,” he says. Think precious jewelry viewed through frosted glass. “The sparkle is still there, but it has this gorgeous soft-focus quality. It doesn’t actually make the manicure matte, but it softens the visual intensity of the glitter, and you can choose a glossy or matte topcoat depending on the final look you want.”

With coffee, Voloshyna prefers a glossy finish to keep the color rich and creamy, like fresh coffee. Matte topcoats are reserved for mocha and espresso manis to give a soft, velvety look. “In winter, a brown cat-eye adds depth and makes the color look like melted chocolate,” she concludes.

#6913. The Hirshhorn’s New Sculpture Garden Is a Vision of America

The Smithsonian institution’s newly transformed outdoor gallery opens as a wider debate over art, history, and the national identity has spilled onto the National Mall in Washington, D.C.

In Washington, D.C., in early September, in West Potomac Park just off the National Mall, workers in a fenced-off section of an athletic field were seen smoothing freshly poured concrete. They were preparing, it turned out, for the installation of two aywans—traditional Uzbek open-sided pavilions, with elaborately carved wooden columns and roofs. The pavilions were a gift from Uzbekistan’s president, Shavkat Mirziyoyev, to President Trump and the American people, presented as a symbol of “friendship and strategic partnership” between the countries amid ongoing trade negotiations and to mark America’s 250th anniversary this year.

The aywans were being installed at the site Trump has designated for his proposed National Garden of American Heroes, a park featuring 250 life-size statues of notable Americans. (In addition to past presidents and political figures, Mark Twain, Harriet Tubman, Martin Luther King Jr., Julia Child, Kobe Bryant, and curiously, the late Canadian-born longtime host of Jeopardy!, Alex Trebek, were among the early names floated.) The federal call for artists specified that the statues in the garden would be realistic and classical, and be made from traditional materials like marble, granite, bronze, copper, or brass. The development is also envisioned to include reflecting pools, plazas, cafés, and an amphitheater.

The National Garden of Heroes remains, officially, a preliminary concept still subject to federal review, with questions over congressional authorization looming—although Uzbekistan’s ambassador has said that the aywans will become part of it.

But in Washington, D.C., a city built on symbolism, the distinction between what is planned and what is already taking shape can be a slippery one. The capital—and the Mall in particular—has always been a place where ideas about what America is and represents are writ especially large. The monuments, memorials, protests, performances, celebrations, and public art have all helped define what we choose to commemorate, project, and remember. As the Trump administration undertakes its own recasting of those civic spaces—from the “American Flag Blue” resurfacing of the Reflecting Pool to the proposed 250-foot arch—the landscape is one where competing visions of America are continuing to be made manifest, often in real time.

A little over a mile northeast of where the aywans were being installed, off the Mall itself, another kind of garden was finding its form. At the Hirshhorn, the Smithsonian’s national museum of modern and contemporary art, the finishing touches were being put on a four-year transformation of the museum’s outdoor sculpture garden, which has been redesigned by the Japanese artist and architect Hiroshi Sugimoto.

The project was the culmination of now former Hirshhorn director Melissa Chiu’s 12-year tenure leading the museum; at the end of August, she left to become director of the Guggenheim in New York. When the garden opens on October 31, it will bring together nearly 60 works spanning more than a century of sculpture, from pieces in founder Joseph H. Hirshhorn’s collection to recent acquisitions and commissions by a diverse range of artists working today. But the garden is also in many ways a distillation of some of the more essential questions that Chiu sought to address during her time at the museum—chiefly around who contemporary art is for, what gets represented in the collection, and how to think about the remit of a national museum in a country as vast and diverse as the United States.

In 1939, Congress called for a museum of contemporary art in Washington, D.C., to complement the more classical National Gallery of Art. The idea was eventually realized in the 1960s, when Smithsonian Secretary S. Dillon Ripley persuaded financier and philanthropist Joseph H. Hirshhorn, a Latvian-born Jewish immigrant who had amassed a significant collection of modern art, to donate his collection. Hirshhorn was particularly interested in 19th- and early 20th-century sculpture, owning major works by Auguste Rodin, Pablo Picasso, Henri Matisse, Alberto Giacometti, Alexander Calder, and Henry Moore, while also collecting the work of then-living American artists like Willem de Kooning, Milton Avery, Stuart Davis, Arshile Gorky, Edward Hopper, and Larry Rivers. An Act of Congress established the Hirshhorn Museum and Sculpture Garden in 1966, and it opened on the Mall in 1974.

Designed by Gordon Bunshaft, the museum was conceived as a piece of sculpture itself—a monumental, elevated concrete cylinder with a sunken garden that broke sharply with the neoclassical architecture of the other institutions around it. Its holdings have since grown to more than 13,000 works, but its mandate remained distinctly forward-looking: to serve as a national museum for the art and artists of its own time.

I spoke with Chiu in late August, as she wound down her time at the Hirshhorn. Washington, D.C., was in the throes of preparing for the Freedom 250 Grand Prix, the IndyCar street race to mark the country’s 250th-anniversary celebrations. (The White House, which worked to clear the way for the race via executive order, described it as an event designed to “showcase the majesty” of Washington and its national monuments.) In the days leading up to the race, the area around the Mall was being transformed into a temporary racetrack: streets were closed, barricades were erected, and increased security disrupted traffic flow through the city. At one point during our conversation, Chiu had to step away to take a call because someone she was expecting was having trouble getting to the museum amid all the obstructions.

The brief interruption offered another D.C.-sized metaphor. The Australia-born Chiu, who spent 13 years at Asia Society in New York before coming to the Hirshhorn in 2014, arrived thinking about what it meant for the institution to be not simply another museum of modern and contemporary art, but a national museum dedicated to that work.

The museum came to refer to its approach as “radical accessibility”; Chiu said the idea was ultimately about more than making contemporary art easier to understand. “We described it much later and articulated it as being artist-centric,” she said. If a visitor was confronted with a seemingly difficult or complex work, the question was how best to help that person appreciate it. “It was really actually the artist,” she said, rather than “an abstracted art-historical explanation.”

That meant reducing the physical and intellectual barriers that might stand between visitors and the artist whose work they were seeing and rethinking everything from the tone of the wall texts and educational materials to the museum’s voice, as well as developing Hirshhorn Eye, a mobile guide that allows visitors to listen to artists talk about their own work. It also meant finding ways to extend the museum’s reach beyond its own walls, through digital projects, video, and even television. (The Exhibit: Finding the Next Great Artist, a six-part competition series, aired on MTV and the Smithsonian Channel in 2023).

The Hirshhorn’s audience is unusually broad for a museum of contemporary art. Because it is free and sits on the Mall, many visitors arrive with little expectation that they are viewing one of the country’s most important collections of modern and contemporary art. Some may be encountering that kind of work for the first time.

“I do think there is a kind of responsibility that we hold to our public given that we are that kind of public institution on the National Mall,” Chiu said. “It’s a different way of being in the world, I think,” she explains. “We tried to be less institutional, less formal.”

The Hirshhorn’s original collection, Chiu said, represented “the absolute canon of sculpture in the 20th century.” But canons today can represent a lot of different things to different people. Chiu’s goal was to ensure that a museum founded to reflect the present truly reflected the way artists now work across disciplines, using new technologies and materials, drawing on different traditions and histories, popular culture, and their own individual backgrounds and experiences. During her tenure, the Hirshhorn mounted major surveys of artists such as Shirin Neshat, Robert Irwin, Yayoi Kusama, Laurie Anderson, and Charline von Heyl. Kusama’s “Infinity Mirrors” show, in particular, became a phenomenon, helping to propel the museum’s annual attendance past one million in 2017.

The new sculpture garden is perhaps the clearest expression of that project. Major works from the original collection will return, including Rodin’s The Burghers of Calais and Monument to Balzac, Matisse’s complete series of The Backs I–IV, and works by Moore, Giacometti, Barbara Hepworth, David Smith, and Lucio Fontana. They will be joined by recent acquisitions and commissions that substantially widen the geographical and cultural frame by artists like Lauren Halsey, Raven Halfmoon, Mark Grotjahn, Liz Larner, Woody De Othello, Izumi Kato, Pedro Reyes, and Chatchai Puipia. Among them: Halfmoon’s Dancing at Dusk, which draws on Caddo traditions and contemporary Native life; Halsey’s keepers of the krown (antoinette grace halsey), a monumental column topped with a portrait of her grandmother; Kato’s Untitled, which transforms stones sourced in Japan into an otherworldly aluminum figure; Puipia’s Wish You Were Here, a reclining bronze wrapped in a traditional Thai pha khao ma cloth; and Pedro Reyes’s Tonatiuh, carved from volcanic stone and connected to pre-Columbian and Modernist Mexican traditions.

The garden will also feature KAWS’s SHELTER, a 14-foot bronze depicting two of his familiar COMPANION figures as parent and child; Yoko Ono’s Wish Tree for Washington, D.C., which invites visitors to leave behind handwritten wishes for peace; and Elmgreen & Dragset’s Eight, a 28-foot-tall vertical swimming pool complete with diving board, ladder, and lights. On the West Lawn, Yoshitomo Nara will inaugurate a new series of commissions with three monumental white heads—Long Tall Peace Sister, Smiling, and Tiny Bright Round Eyes—enlargements of small clay heads he made in 2021. In the aftermath of the 2011 Tōhoku earthquake and Fukushima nuclear disaster, Nara found himself unable to paint and turned to clay.

The original garden, designed by Bunshaft, was spare, recessed, and largely devoid of shade or greenery. Bunshaft had emulated the Zen gardens he visited in Japan, creating an austere setting in which the sculptures could stand apart from their surroundings. But it wasn’t exactly welcoming. Visitors entering from the Mall had to descend a flight of stairs, only to encounter a 10-foot concrete wall. Within a decade, landscape architect Lester Collins was brought in to soften the space, adding canopy trees, more vegetation, additional seating, and a ramp. But by the 2010s the garden was also contending with deteriorating infrastructure and leaks in the passageway that connected it to the museum plaza. More fundamentally, it had become increasingly ill-equipped for the scale and variety of work the museum was presenting.

To remake it, Chiu turned to Sugimoto, who already had a long relationship with the Hirshhorn, which presented a survey of his work in 2006. They had already collaborated on a redesign of the museum’s lobby, unveiled in 2018, which sought to work with Bunshaft’s architecture rather than overwrite it.

Sugimoto approached the garden as a place for people to engage with art rather than simply an architectural intervention. He widened its entrance and brought it up to the same grade as the Mall. He created long sightlines and a sequence of open and intimate spaces defined by a series of dry-stacked stone walls constructed from roughly 950 tons of stone. He also reopened the passageway connecting the garden to the museum plaza, transforming it into a sculptural tunnel.

Sugimoto said his goal was to work in dialogue with Bunshaft’s original design rather than erase it. “Architecture must reflect the spirit of the age in which it was built,” he explained. “That is what makes a great work of architecture endure in history.” His aim was to revive and expand some of the original ideas behind Bunshaft’s garden by looking backward as well as forward. “In considering what backdrop would best suit the viewing of modern sculpture,” he said, “I concluded that it should be a pre-modern one.”

He also reinterpreted the Japanese karesansui, or dry landscape garden, where minimalist arrangements of rocks and raked gravel evoke mountains and water. “It is expressed as a void within space, suggesting presence through absence,” he explained. “Rather than flaunting monumentality through size or height, I intended a serene space that sits quietly buried within its environment.”

The works on the West Lawn will change annually, while performances, educational programs, and other activities keep the space in motion. “It really is an invitation,” Chiu said of the redesign. “It’s an invitation to come inside.” The wider entrance from the Mall and the at-grade approach are a big part of that, allowing visitors to enter more easily and establishing a different relationship between the art and the audience. “Lauren Halsey’s grandmother will be at eye level with them now.”

The garden is a distillation of what Chiu said she tried to do throughout her time at the Hirshhorn: preserve the museum’s inheritance while expanding the definition of who, what, and whose histories belong in the collection—and, more broadly, what it means to be a national museum of contemporary art in a country like the United States

That question has taken on a new kind of urgency amid the Trump administration’s ongoing attempt to influence the way the Smithsonian institutions present American history and culture. Over the last year and a half, the administration has repeatedly threatened to withhold federal funding and other forms of government support over what it considers ideologically slanted material. This past summer, the White House released a 162-page report accusing the National Museum of American History of “extreme political activism” and objecting to what it characterized as a presentation of the country through “regret, tragedy, and shame.” Officials were directed to install temporary signs outside the museum warning visitors about what the administration deemed “inaccurate information” about American history.

In September, Trump called for the Smithsonian to install an 11-foot statue of George Washington in the National Museum of American History’s Flag Hall and, more strikingly, to replace Infinity, a 1967 abstract sculpture by José de Rivera outside the museum, with a 30-foot statue of Washington. He also called for a five-year exhibition devoted to Washington leading up to the 300th anniversary of his birth in 2032. “Replace a sculpture that says nothing about America with the man who, more than anyone else, says everything about America,” Trump posted on Truth Social (on September 11). Infinity is often said to be the first contemporary sculpture permanently installed on the Mall and, at the time, was the largest piece of contemporary sculpture ever commissioned by the federal government.

Earlier that month, Smithsonian Secretary Lonnie G. Bunch III announced he would retire at the end of the year after nearly four decades at the institution and seven years as secretary. Bunch said his decision was not related to the pressure from the administration, but his departure comes as the Smithsonian is navigating an increasingly public debate over its independence and authority.

Chiu, who I spoke with before Bunch’s announcement, was careful not to characterize the moment simply as an attack on museums, but part of a larger shift in the relationship between museums and the public. “I would say that over my tenure, I’ve seen different groups provide feedback on what they think museums should be doing and what they think museums should be showing, and that feedback is much more visible today, because a visitor can leave a message in the comments or post on social media,” she said. “Before, there was no feedback loop, no feedback mechanism, so I’d say that that has been going on for close to the last decade, actually—and yes, the past 18 months, it’s been a different kind of feedback loop that is very much about how Smithsonian museums present American history.”

She did not see that heightened scrutiny simply as a threat. “The fact that people care enough to voice concern and commitment about what their museums are doing, how they’re presenting the artifacts or artworks, shows that it matters,” she said.

I asked Chiu if the responsibility she referred to earlier was different for a national museum in a country as vast and diverse as the U.S. than for a private one. “I think that if you’re a public museum, you receive funding from public entities and from the government, and so with that, there are certain responsibilities,” she said. “One of them is that we’re free. Another is that we’re open every day but one throughout the entire year. And then the other, at least in the way that we think about it at the Hirshhorn, is that we are representative of different views by artists.”

In Washington, D.C., public sculpture has traditionally been used to declare, to commemorate, to fix history in place. The Hirshhorn’s new garden, Chiu suggested, reflects a country whose cultural history has never belonged to a single tradition. “I think perhaps here in America, more than anywhere else, this has been a country of many stories,” she told me. “I see what we’ve done in the sculpture garden as being representative of this country, that it was always one that welcomed artists from everywhere.”

As the Hirshhorn’s new sculpture garden prepares to open this weekend just days ahead of the midterm elections on November 3, the landscape around it continues to evolve. Earlier this month, the National Park Service unveiled a revised plan for the Tidal Basin and West Potomac Park that includes a new Great Lawn and Oval Park near the site that has been proposed for the National Garden of American Heroes. The garden itself is not part of the Park Service’s current plan, officials said, but preservationists immediately questioned whether the new configuration could ultimately make room for it.

I asked Chiu what she hoped to find when she returned to the Hirshhorn. “The best possible thing as a museum director that you can see is people,” she said. “Just people enjoying art.”

#6912. Newly Minted Mr. Darcy, Jack Lowden, Responds to James Bond Short-List Rumors

Nothing more iconic than going from Mr. Darcy to 007.

We’re this close to finding out who the next James Bond is, and the short list? Callum Turner and Jack Lowden—the latter of whom is in the middle of a truly iconic run in Slow Horses, not to mention about to play Mr. Darcy in Netflix’s upcoming Pride & Prejudice adaptation.

According to The Hollywood Reporter, “several sources” confirmed that final screen tests for the Denis Villeneuve-directed film were taking place in London last week, which…presumably means Bond has been chosen. Cut to Jack, who was asked about the rumors in a recent GQ interview.

“On this… It’s all very flattering,” he said. “It’s nice to be mentioned—for any role—and I’m just not really going to add to the noise.”

Speaking specifically of his Slow Horses costar Gary Oldman saying he hoped Jack would get the part, he said, “It’s very nice, and Gary’s a sweetheart—it’s particularly lovely, coming from someone like him. But I don’t really listen to it. As a young male actor, generally, the minute you’re not playing a zany artist type, the minute you’re in a suit, people fling it at you.”

Not exactly a denial! Meanwhile, Callum told THR over the summer, “I know as much as you do—really, I know as much as you do. I’ll tell you what’s so funny about the Bond thing: Even your best friends ask you, people text you that you haven’t spoken to for 10 years—and you know nothing! It’s such a weird thing of something happening and nothing happening at all. I genuinely know nothing. I just find it quite amusing.”

Honestly, I’d take either of them as Bond. Or both! Just happy to be here.

#6906. My Two Wedding Dresses Were a Love Letter to My Haitian Roots and New Ghanaian Family

I was looking for just one perfect gown, but what I got was a beautiful story of bridging heritage, family, and belonging. (And of course, two absolutely stunning custom pieces.)

I thought I would cry. There I was perched prettily on a podium in a gorgeous champagne wedding dress and mantilla veil. My mom and three sisters beamed up at me in between their sips of bubbly, awaiting my enthusiastic proclamation that this dress was, much like my fiancé, The One.

A soft violin rendition of an inspirational song slowly crescendoed through the speakers, and it all felt like signs that this was the gown I’d say “I do” in. I stared at myself in the mirror almost blankly, tilted my head, and asked my family, “Really? This is beautiful but is it me?” I tried to find the words to explain to my heart-eyed audience that there were no butterflies, no fireworks, no cinematic overture, no little voice spelling out yes.

I waited, hoping the rush that was supposed to sweep me away was a little off schedule. But...nothing. Isn't this the part where I’m supposed to burst into tears? I couldn’t rationalize how all of the frenzy and booked appointments came down to this level of underwhelm. I left without a dress that day, and even though my wedding was in a mere two months (yes, really), I wasn't panicked—yet.

I know what you’re thinking: “Isn’t it a little late to be wedding dress shopping?” And normally, you’d be right. But after getting engaged in November 2025, my fiancé and I decided to skip the long engagement. There was just one tiny detail: We had two months to plan the entire wedding, which meant finding the dress of my dreams was officially a race against the clock.

Unfortunately, this dress scenario played out several more times. By the time I got to Kleinfeld Bridal, the iconic New York City shop featured on TLC’s Say Yes to the Dress, I was positive I would find the perfect wedding dress there. I felt confident, primed to cancel future dress appointments, telling myself this day would end with the happy tears and excitement that I dreamt of as a little girl playing dress up. My mom and sisters were aligned, and kept their eyes open for any “wow” moments. Then my stylist brought out an every-bride-would-love-this custom Pnina Tornai gown that had a compelling backstory. A sucker for some good lore, but not an emotional buyer, I was okay with not having it. Once again, I left another bridal salon dress-less.

All of this had me perplexed: I loved how I looked in every gown, but it felt like it was something bigger missing and made me consider meeting with a therapist to figure it out. Was it wedding jitters? I dissected myself after every boutique visit, and often wondered, “What’s wrong with me?” Conscious of the clock, part of me wanted to just pick one and keep the peace. Appointment after appointment, maybe I was too difficult to please? But I refused to talk myself into a dress—I am allergic to settling, after all.

I grew up Haitian-American, and in my family, there was a real appreciation for presentation and what you wore. You dressed up for church, celebrations, and even going to the grocery store. Don’t get me started on school picture day. Even in college, while my classmates wore sweats to class, I’d be pumping through campus in heels. My family instilled in me that you showed up looking good because looking good was part of showing up. It was more than superficial; looking good was a way of showing respect, taking pride in yourself, and representing your family with honor. So I wasn't just trying to find a beautiful gown. Before I took on a new last name, filled with new traditions, I needed to recognize myself—where I come from—in it.

Everything shifted on a solo trip to San Francisco. I was luxuriating in bed at the Palace Hotel when a thought landed on me like fresh dew in the morning: I, a Haitian-American fashion girlie, was marrying a Ghanaian artist—I was entering a marriage that brought two cultures, two families, and two creative identities together. What if I wear a Haitian designer for the ceremony, and then honor his culture with a Ghanaian traditional gown during the reception? I thought. Finally, the feeling I had been searching for started coursing through my body.

For my Haitian piece, I began researching and it ended almost instantaneously when I stumbled upon Jean-Ralph Thurin, a Haitian designer I was already following on Instagram. We met years ago during NYFW when I was getting my start in the industry. I reached out and scheduled an appointment for as soon as I landed back on the East coast.

Jean-Ralph’s atelier was elevated, with cream, ivory, and white gowns displayed throughout the luscious showroom; each one reminded me of some special element of Haiti. The loveseats created an intimate, almost familial feeling, like you’d come over to someone’s beautiful living room and settled in to watch a show.

The family vibes only intensified as the appointment went on. Jean-Ralph’s wife, a fellow designer, helped me in and out of dresses, and his daughter was his attendant. It felt like being at my aunt’s house, and suddenly it made sense why none of the other shops worked for me—I was meant to be here.

As my sisters oohed and ahhed at me in a dress that I couldn’t stop smiling in, Jean-Ralph sketched an entirely new option for me in his notepad. It was a rendition of what I had on (which was sadly too big and couldn’t be altered due to the elaborate detailing) and totally me. “I’m inspired,” He told me. “I’m going to design this for you.” I was honored. It’s like he saw past the weeks of hidden disappointment I’d been hiding behind my smile, and he knew there was a story begging to be told through my dress.

With just five weeks until the wedding, I held my breath through every fitting, clinging to hope. The gown, which was for the ceremony, had a pearl-beaded lace bodice in a warmer ivory champagne. The moment I put it on, everyone—my mom, my sisters, and yes, myself—said it: “This is the one.” I never felt so sure, so complete. I felt like me: the once-shy Haitian-American girl who grew up to be a woman of the world.

And yet, we were only halfway there. For my second look, my fiancé’s entire family helped bring it to life: His brother, a menswear designer in Ghana, connected me to a talented seamstress; his sister bought the material; and his mom searched for the accessories and we discovered renowned Ampy Bridal fans. One WhatsApp call after the other, an ocean away, my gown came together: a vibrant purple and fuchsia ombré, complemented by rich kente detailing, glistening stones, and flowery embellishments.

When it finally arrived, I knew this wasn’t something even a fairy godmother could have conjured. As the first child and eldest daughter of immigrant parents, I was used to forging the path and getting things done. This time, it was a family affair, and I was catching a glimpse of who I was becoming. What started as a dress became something much bigger: a beautiful merging of my Haitian heritage and his Ghanaian roots, stitched together by the family I was becoming part of. And somehow, before we even said “I do,” I already felt like I belonged.

And then, after a dizzying two months, there I was: walking down the aisle feeling like royalty in my custom Haitian designer gown. Every emotion came flooding to the surface. The butterflies I’d been waiting for were swirling in my stomach. At the reception, I changed into my one-of-a-kind traditional Ghanaian dress, dancing towards my husband as every piece of me was on full, beautiful display: a Haitian-American, a new wife, a woman who loves a Ghanaian man and his culture. My Haitian gown let me honor where I came from; my Ghanaian gown let me honor the culture I was marrying into. And together, they felt like coming home.

#6904. Two-Tone Heels Are the Playful Shoe Trend Your Fall Wardrobe Needs

Add a bold, quirky finish to your monochrome outfits.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Whether your wardrobe is ready or not, one thing is clear: designers want us to wear more color. The message came through on several spring/summer 2027 runways, including Carolina Herrera, LII, and Miu Miu. Footwear is where the idea gets especially playful.

Two-tone shoes have been trending all year, largely thanks to Matthieu Blazy’s work at Chanel, where bicolor heels on the fall 2026 runway showed that fashion doesn’t have to take itself so seriously. A little quirkiness is the whole point. Bold yet unexpected, the two-tone shoe is a fun surprise against an otherwise tonal outfit.

The best part is how easy it is to try. You don’t need to rebuild your closet or commit to a head-to-toe color story. Start at your feet by pairing a split-color heel with a monochrome look, and let the shoes be the only loud note. If you want to go further, pick one shade from the shoe and echo it in a bag, a scarf, or a lip color. The contrast does the work, and the outfit looks intentional rather than overthought.

Below, the best two-tone heels to shop for fall, from a splurge-worthy designer pair to options that won’t strain your budget.

A dusty blue and brown pairing is a surprisingly perfect combination. Built on an already appealing silhouette, this mule brings effortless energy and everyday sophistication.

Not quite sold on color? This brown and black pair plays it muted, but the glossy patent leather gives it plenty of dimension.

This classic black and white pairing will do wonders for your wardrobe. It’s the kind of timeless shoe you’ll reach for again and again.

We’ve always loved a bold red and purple pairing, and this wedge, with its statuesque heel and sleek pointed toe, is meant for making an entrance.

There’s something sweet and innocent about these burgundy-pink Mary Janes that softens even the sharpest, most structured pieces.

The color division may not be obvious, but these square-toe pumps still make a solid statement by pairing a satin leopard print with a shiny patent toe cap.

With a square toe and V-shaped topline, this heel already says modern and sophisticated, but the contrasting white toe cap adds even more stylish flair.

Despite being light on color, this toned-down slingback proves that a neutral combo never feels outdated when the shoe shape reads timeless.

#6899. Beyoncé Shares Rare Photos With Blue Ivy Carter Backstage at Jay-Z’s L.A. Concert

On October 24, Blue Ivy Carter joined her father, Jay-Z, onstage for the first night of his Jay-Z 30 L.A. concert, playing the piano as he performed. Her mother, Beyoncé, watched in the crowd—and posted her own personal photos from the evening on her site this week. In one shot, Beyoncé and Blue appear together with Beyoncé’s mom, Tina Knowles, backstage.

Beyoncé also posted plenty of outfit shots, putting the spotlight on her Roberto Cavalli look and Messika jewelry. The ensemble featured a white fitted froissé silk satin “Orchidea” print top and an emerald tulle pareo skirt. The bottom was fully embroidered with matching sequins and jet-bead fringes, per the fashion house. See all the photos on Beyoncé’s site.

Jay-Z spoke earlier this year about Blue’s musical talent, revealing she is a very gifted keyboardist. “Blue is a crazy pianist, but she won’t let us get her a teacher,” he told GQ in March. “She doesn’t want it to be a job. But she has perfect pitch. If she hears a song, she’ll be like, ‘Play it again,’ and then she’ll teach herself. That’s just talent; she doesn’t work at that. She worked at this [performing as a dancer during Beyoncé’s Cowboy Carter Tour], and it makes me proud that she fought for something that she really wanted to do. I don’t think we’re going to be able to get her off that stage now.”

Two weeks ago, Blue joined Beyoncé onstage for the opening of the Knowles-Rowland House in Houston. She cut the ribbon with her mom and younger sister Rumi.

#6898. This Designer’s Parisian Pied-à-terre Features a Palette As Bold As It Is Beautiful

A storied apartment found new life through beloved heirlooms and a reverence for the past.

Paris, in Garrow Kedigian’s telling, looks better in color. Not the cream, black, and gilt version that has come to dominate our collective imagination, but cognac, lacquered red, turquoise, and umber—the type of palette that makes a room feel alive before even a single antique arrives. “Traditionally, French design was very colorful,” Kedigian says, citing the richly layered interiors of François Catroux and Jacques Grange as antecedents. “It was only in the modern age that white and cream has just overtaken.”

His own Paris apartment, on Rue de Varenne in the 7th arrondissement, makes a persuasive case for the pigment of a bygone era. Located directly across from the Musée Rodin, the home is a step up—literally and figuratively—from Kedigian’s former pied-â-terre, a smaller space that proved a little too successful at attracting visiting friends and family. “I ended up giving them my bed, and then I’d have to sleep on the sofa,” he recalls. More time in Paris also meant more work there, and the designer wanted a proper desk, rather than an “office” that disappeared every time he cleared the dining table for guests.

What he found boasted grandeur in spades—14-foot ceilings, generous rooms, an enviable address—but had suffered through an unfortunate 1970s renovation. Kedigian, who has a background in classical architecture, set about putting the apartment back together, basing much of his approach on one of the few surviving details: a curved bay window and the kitchen’s shapely cabinetry. “That was the generator for the apartment,” Kedigian says. From there, he opened up sight lines, raised the doorways to 19 feet, restored missing moldings, and sourced period-appropriate details, like a Pompadour-style mantel, at Parisian flea markets.

His studied mix of restrained maximalism is on display in every corner. Antiques gathered across decades mingle with modern art, Venetian lighting, sculptural objects, and sentimental pieces from former homes. It’s a chemistry lab of sorts, a place Kedigian refers to as his “prototype,” where he can try the moves a client might initially resist. And yet, the result is undeniably cohesive, neither perfectly preserved nor particularly interested in behaving itself. As Kedigian puts it, “It’s mixing the things that you find most beautiful in a way that they work well together.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Garrow KedigianStylist: Robert RufinoLocation: Paris, FranceThe Space: A two-bedroom, two-and-a-half-bathroom apartment across 1450 square feet

Affectionately dubbed the “Grand Salon,” this elegant space pays homage to all the things designer Garrow Kedigian loves about Parisian design, from the one-of-a-kind blue mixed for the walls to the intricate plaster molding and the custom tufted banquette that follows the window’s curve.

“The original plaster crown molding, fireplace mantle, and over mantle mirror really anchor the room,” says Kedigian. “My furniture layout plan was a derivative of the fireplace and curved window, including banquette seating organized around the fireplace.”

Kedigian prefers to work surrounded by red, though this custom Benjamin Moore paint shade is decidedly more “fiery” than the one in his New York City apartment. “The walls were a custom mixed color—I wanted a rich, enveloping red with a lot of energy, hence more towards the orange tones,” he adds. Built-ins anchor one side of the room, while large-scale dentil molding frames the space, adding contrast and texture.

This passageway is a main artery through the home, so Kedigian knew it needed to carry its weight. To make it purposeful, he relocated several doors and added a curved niche to spotlight favorite objets d’art.

Anchored by a vintage La Cornue stove (a discontinued colorway Kedigian found on Le Bon Coin, the French eBay), the blue and red kitchen nods to the French flag, giving Kedigian a stylish spot for pre-dinner cocktails (though, admittedly, not much cooking). “Kitchen should always be designed as an extension of your main living spaces; colors and materials should be carried throughout,” says Kedigian.

Spurred on by a bold black-and-mustard color palette—which a client of Kedigian’s fell for after seeing it in his space—the cozy dining nook leans into drama, backed by an expansive mirror and a gilded ceiling that bounce light around the room.

In what Kedigian calls his “greatest trick,” he painted double lines onto the walls to mimic the look of molding where there is none. A curtained 18th-century canopy bed brings historic flair.

Fulfilling his love for a bathroom that is “sultry and sexy,” Kedigian clad this space in a striking mix of black marble and gleaming brass sheets.

Born and raised in Montreal, interior designer Garrow Kedigian studied architecture at McGill University before honing his design sensibility under celebrated Boston designer William Hodgins. After relocating to New York City, he established his eponymous firm in 2001, building a reputation for richly layered interiors that marry classical architecture with bold color, sophisticated detailing, and contemporary flair. His portfolio spans residential and commercial projects across Manhattan, Greenwich, and Palm Beach, including his own storied Park Avenue apartment, once home to Fred Astaire.

#6893. This Heritage Pattern Is Replacing Flat, Beige Walls—Here’s How Designers Are Using It in 2026

The centuries-old motif also makes lampshades, rugs, and seating feel bespoke.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

In an age of encroaching AI, designers are turning to heritage looks to create bespoke interiors with personality. That may be one reason decorative marbling is having a moment.

"People are tired of flat, safe walls,” says designer Cara Woodhouse. “After years of greige, clients want surfaces with a point of view, and marbling has an artisanal history behind it. It's also flexible: it can read painterly, graphic, or soft depending on the colorway."

Its versatility is part of the appeal. “It doesn't necessarily have to be traditional or even executed on paper,” adds designer Sasha Bikoff. “It's really about the movement of color and the organic, unpredictable patterns that emerge, which can translate beautifully across wallpaper, furniture, textiles, and decorative objects.”

The paper marbling technique has a long history. In 10th-century Japan, artisans created “floating ink” designs by placing ink on water and manipulating it as it moved. By the 15th century, the practice was known in Turkey as “the art of clouds,” before making its way to Venetian and Florentine bookbinders.

The painterly look is appealing because its undulating lines feel expressive and vibrant without harsh edges, says Jo Plant, chief creative officer at Pooky, which is a brand well-known for its marbled lampshades.

Designers are bringing marbling into all kinds of interiors, from murals and upholstery to lampshades and even sink skirts. Analisse Taft-Gersten, cofounder of the 1818 Collective, used billowy marbled silk to elevate a powder room. And studios like Rule of Three are experimenting with the technique in floral fabrics that resemble vines.

Should you need further convincing, Woodhouse puts it best: "Marbling is pattern without repetition. No two passes are the same, so a wall reads more like art than wallpaper. It brings depth and a handmade quality to a room that might otherwise feel too finished."

Here are nine designer-approved ways to bring marbled motifs into your home.

Designer Michelle Fahmy of Haus of Meeshie likes things cheeky and playful. So it was a no-brainer to introduce groovy swirls into a Culver City pied-à-terre via a large-scale mural from Mitchell Black.

“I wanted something dramatic for the stairwell wall which bleeds into the living room and is the first thing you see when you walk in the door,” she says. “When I came across this wallpaper, it stopped me in my tracks! Usually, you see this paper marbling technique done on fabric or in smaller applications, like a lampshade, so this was unusual in scale and exactly the conversation starter I was looking for.”

For Bikoff, interior design should feel like a natural extension of a home’s architectural language. To bring her own sense of color, movement, and personality into a project within a Zaha Hadid building, she opted for Pierre Frey wallpaper with a liquid, dripping marble effect rather than a traditional, tightly swirled pattern.

“I love how the elongated, flowing lines create a sense of movement, almost as though the walls are melting or the colors are cascading down the surface,” Bikoff says. “Traditional marbling can feel very classical, whereas this interpretation feels more contemporary, psychedelic, and expressive.”

The dialogue between the wallpaper and Hadid's architecture is all about challenging conventional forms and embracing organic movement. “I wanted the room to feel immersive, almost like stepping inside a piece of abstract art,” she adds.

It was love at first sight. Marta St. Amour, interior designer and project manager at Braswell Design + Build was enchanted by the beautiful movement and layered tones of amber, soft blue, and sandy beige portrayed in the Lee Jofa Taplow wallpaper.

“The pattern became the inspiration for the bathroom's color palette, tying together the warm veining of Taj Mahal quartzite, rich caramel-colored shower tile, and soft blue tile behind the freestanding tub,” she says.

“We wanted the tub to feel like a statement piece, and the wallpaper's organic, flowing pattern complements its sculptural shape and warm metallic finish.”

To capture the spirit of the marbling technique but in a contemporary way, Bikoff opted for resin chairs with the telltale pattern in the above space.

“The way the colors blend and swirl through the resin gives them this incredible sense of depth and movement, almost like a liquid material that has been frozen in time,” she says.

Juxtaposing materials can challenge the perception of design. Here, the chairs feel like functional sculptures as they balance the visual richness of marble and the playful transparency of resin.

“They bring together color, texture, and fluidity while reinforcing the playful, futuristic spirit of the space."

If a full room of swirls is a bit much for you, settle on something smaller-scale, such as a lampshade. Pooky’s hand-marbled shades show off the dance of acrylic paint.

“Marbling is having a resurgence because we’re all craving craft, texture, and character in our homes,” says Plant. “People want pieces that feel human and handmade rather than mass-produced.” Adding a marbled shade is like decor candy that can add just the right amount of maximalism.

What makes the marbling technique unique is that the patterns are inherently organic, and so they lend themselves to pairing well with other organic shapes. Taft-Gersten anchored this powder room with a silk sink skirt featuring Rule of Three fabric that almost looks like it was kissed by bubbles.

“There’s a rhythm to it, but also an irregularity that keeps your eye moving; it never feels rigid,” Taft-Gersten says. The print also echoes the convex pebble mirrors above.

This girl’s bedroom may look dreamy, but that’s not what Cara Woodhouse was after when she landed on cotton candy-like Merenda Wallpaper by artist Michelle Weinberg.

“I wanted movement,” Woodhouse says. “A kid's room should feel alive, and marbled paper gives the walls energy without a single literal motif. I'd call it soft modern with an edge: rounded forms, blush tones, and a pattern that keeps it from going saccharine. The swirl does the work so the furniture can stay simple and sculptural."

The look of poured paint over a rug is one that will surely raise an eyebrow. Floor_Story designer Bekah Smith was experimenting with duct tape when she stumbled upon unexpected patterns that look like creases and shadows.

“There’s a similar sense of spontaneity to marbling, where patterns emerge through experimentation rather than being entirely predetermined,” Smith says.

For those looking for a ripple effect without the psychedelic swirls, consider ceramics as a way to dabble in the style without being so literal. Henry Holland Studio employs a traditional Japanese ceramic technique, nerikomi, to create irregular earthenware marbled motifs.

The pattern is created by stacking, folding, and rolling colored clay into slabs, then hand-molding them into functional shapes like lamp bases, bowls, and vases.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

Turns out holiday cheer comes with tasting notes.

The Love Is Blind reunion airs Sunday, November 8 at 9 p.m. ET on Netflix. In other words, you don’t have to wait an entire week after the final episodes drop to find out where everyone stands.

Nope! But it was filmed relatively recently!

#6889. The Art of the Evening Sweater: 5 Silhouettes to Wear All Winter Long

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

These sumptuous knits are anything but frumpy.

While I love nothing more than burrowing into my most oversized, shapeless cashmere pullover during the colder months, I admit that it doesn’t feel like the most appropriate sartorial choice for a night out. When I want to feel sexy without spending the whole night shivering, I reach for the unsung hero of my winter wardrobe: the evening sweater.

This season, we’ve seen sexy knitwear in the form of cutout cable knits at Schiaparelli and see-through cardigans at Tom Ford—and the trend translates seamlessly off the runway, too. For the most wearable option, look for a knit with a single element that leans more sultry: a draped neckline that falls off the shoulder, a wrap silhouette that hugs the waist, or a deep plunge that shows off some décolletage. Ahead, see the evening sweater trends to shop for the colder days ahead.

With its ballerina-inspired silhouette, a wrap sweater offers timeless appeal. Alaïa’s and & Other Stories’s takes on the style offer an adjustable wrap design, while Naked Cashmere’s rendition adds a gold-toned pin closure for an elevated touch.

There’s something undeniably sensual about a top that exposes your collarbone, especially in the form of plush cashmere and merino wool knitwear. Whether you prefer Staud’s slouchy one-shoulder pick or Simkhai’s halter straps, these styles have a come-hither effect without trying too hard.

A daring neckline is a surefire way to heat up your cold-weather ensembles, and we saw down-to-there sweaters dominate the runways at Zimmermann and Prada. From Reformation’s chunky pullover to Same’s barrel silhouette, these knits pair nicely with your laciest bra.

Backless sweaters are an unexpected pick for date nights, especially when contrasted with a higher neckline. Standout picks from Lisa Yang, Aritzia, and Tove appear deceptively demure from the front for maximum impact.

For a less bundled-up option, reach for a sleeveless sweater. A halterneck silhouette offers an unexpected take on fall’s most ubiquitous layer while still fitting comfortably beneath a leather jacket, and Ossou’s slinky Halo Top is a perfect match for low-rise denim and a faux fur coat.
