#6851. 23 of Heidi Klum’s Most Iconic Halloween Costumes Through the Years

Her severed hand costume is just the latest jaw-dropping one in her history.

No one does Halloween quite like Heidi Klum. Since 2000, the supermodel has been throwing yearly Halloween parties where celebs gather to mingle and have fun. Just as exciting as her annual bashes, however, are the truly over-the-top costumes she dreams up. Each year, Klum arrives in a costume more elaborate and incredible than the last—it’s truly a sight to see.

Ahead of Halloween, we took a walk down memory lane and rounded up some of the legend’s most memorable costume ensembles throughout the years. From adorable recreations of cartoon characters to fantastical superhero outfits, here are some of our favorites.

For her first-ever Halloween party, Klum wore a striking leather outfit that included a black wig, spiked collar, and knee-high boots. Her red lips and voluminous lashes were also nice touches.

The supermodel pulled a Bianca Jagger and arrived at the function in 2001 dressed up as the 13th-century horse-riding legend Lady Godiva. Her all-white ensemble featured a silver sequined bodysuit paired with a long bleached blonde wig.

The stunning look was created by designer Marc Bouwer. Here, Klum smiles during a fitting for the creation.

In 2002, the star decided on an adorable Betty Boop costume with a strappy red dress, red stilettos, elbow-length gloves, and suitably extravagant makeup. Her Halloween party that year was held at Capitale restaurant in New York City.

She’s pictured here posing with her fellow Victoria’s Secret Angel, Karolina Kurkova, while at the festivities.

For her 2003 holiday fête, the supermodel chose a gold alienesque look which showcased a metallic headpiece and gold metal teeth.

Klum showed up to her bash dressed in a witchy red look that consisted of a waist-length red wig, voluminous pointed hat, bone accessories, and plenty of red eye makeup. The party was held at Marquee in New York City that year.

She wore another spooky look for her 2005 Halloween get-together held in Happy Valley, New York. It’s hard to recognize her between the gigantic black wig and the pointy fangs.

The model attended her 7th annual Halloween party dressed as the forbidden apple (snake headdress and apple dress included) from the biblical story of Adam and Eve. Her then-husband, Seal, showed up as Eve.

In 2007, she stole attention at her get-together dressed in a head-to-toe cat ensemble which consisted of a gray leotard, bedazzled collar, and lifelike whiskers. The party kicked off in Los Angeles that year.

She’s pictured here testing the look a few days before the festivities.

A few years later, Klum was photographed on the red carpet walking into her event dressed as a superhero. The super original look showcased towering platform shoes, an elbow-length red wig, and all-over purple sparkles.

Klum shocked everyone when she opted to wear a skintight costume that transformed her into a skinless body. To make the look even more convincing, she was wheeled onto the red carpet on a stretcher with a “surgeon” by her side.

She flashed a smile at the camera while getting fitted a month or so before the event.

For her 13th Halloween party, Klum had a team of designers custom make her Cleopatra costume. She offered fans a sneak peek ahead of the finished product.

Klum decided to travel into the future and show up to her 2013 Halloween celebration dressed up as a much older version of herself. She donned a gray wig, a walking cane, and a strand of pearls for the occasion.

Klum wowed partygoers when she chose an intricate and hyper-realistic butterfly costume for her 2014 event.

Here’s a closer look at her awesome face paint that year.

The supermodel pulled out all the stops when she arrived at her 2015 party wearing a Jessica Rabbit costume. Between the lacy red dress, purple gloves, and makeup, Klum looks just like the iconic character.

Klum worked with talented makeup artists to bring her look to the next level.

#6848. 8 Chocolate Advent Calendars That Make a Great Holiday Gift

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

December has enough obligations. You have gifts to buy, travel plans to make, and the office holiday party your boss put on your calendar. How do you fight back? How do you reclaim a second, even half of a second, for yourself? We suggest starting with an Advent calendar. Specifically a chocolate Advent calendar.

But forget about the flimsy ones you might’ve had as a kid with the same mediocre chocolate behind every flap; we’re talking luxurious chocolate truffles and beautifully designed calendars here. Think colorful, handcrafted chocolates filled with the most delicious ganache and boxes so beautiful you might even want to keep them long after the holidays are over. We do also have a few inexpensive options you can easily order from Amazon, in case you aren’t looking for anything too fancy.

Whether it’s for you, your kids, or another special someone in your life, these chocolate Advent calendars won’t just make their day once—they’ll do it 24 days in a row.

Of all the chocolate Advent calendars we tested, Dandelion’s was the one that truly blew us away with its craft and beauty. Packaged in a round blue box with gorgeous silver holiday-themed designs are 25 small round containers that double as tree ornaments. I don’t even have a tree to put these on, but I kept a few ornaments anyway just because they’re so beautiful. And what’s inside each box is the best part—the chocolates.

Dandelion partnered with 24 chocolatiers from across North America and Japan to create the most beautiful, intricately designed, and—most importantly—delicious chocolate confections. To make the experience even more special, it comes with an illustrated storybook with tales from Nordic mythology and folklore, and you get access to daily videos in which Thomas DuBois, an expert in Nordic astral lore, shares the mythology behind each day’s tale.

Shipping: Available for preorder now, ships in November.

Another chocolate calendar that combines gorgeous illustrations with scrumptious chocolate truffles is the new Enchanted Castle Advent calendar from Compartés. You may already be familiar with Compartés for its famous chocolate bars, so it’s no surprise that the brand’s chocolate truffles are equally as delicious. With flavors like Buttered Croissant, Pistachio Marzipan, and Lemon Olive Oil Cake, each door holds a wonderfully sweet surprise. I had to stop myself from eating them all at once. And if you’re getting it as a gift for a lucky someone, you can add on gift wrap and a hand-tied ribbon for a few extra dollars.

Shipping: Available for preorder now, shipping starts November 2.

We can’t talk about chocolate Advent calendars without mentioning Godiva. You know her, you love her. This year’s Godiva Advent calendar features a range of chocolate treats from dark chocolate with caramel to milk chocolate with ganache to raspberry-filled white chocolate stars. My mouth is watering just thinking about these chocolates! It’s really the best you’ll get for under $50.

Another favorite of the Esquire editors this season is this super cool spinning chocolate calendar from La Maison du Chocolat. Each chocolate hides inside a small box, and each box is arranged around this sculptural piece that blends Parisian art and design. I don’t want to give it all away, but I’ll just say my favorite pieces were the milk chocolate almond-hazelnut praline filled with caramelized sesame and the dark chocolate mini bar with crushed crepe dentelle and passionfruit white chocolate.

Shipping: Available for preorder now, ships in November.

Associate Commerce Editor Bryn Gelbart has already written a whole love letter to his Hotel Chocolat Velvetiser hot chocolate maker, so when we found out Hotel Chocolat makes a whole Advent calendar filled with hot chocolate mixes, we knew we had to try it out. And in a big shock to absolutely no one, we’re obsessed. We’ve been leaning into the season already with hot cocoa at the office; some of our favorite blends include the Black Forest Gateau, Chili, and the Classic 70% Dark Chocolate.

Like Godiva, Lindt is an absolute classic when it comes to chocolate Advent calendars. In fact, Lindt’s Holiday Teddy Bear calendar may be one of the most recognizable chocolate Advent calendars out there—and at just $14, you can get one for everyone in the family. Perfect for the person who appreciates a good ol’ milk chocolate candy.

Forgo your morning cup of coffee and start each December day with a piece of caffeinated chocolate from Awake. Once you get a taste of the Plain Milk, Caramel Milk, Peanut Butter Milk, and Salted Caramel Dark Chocolate bites, you’ll want to have these on hand for your next 3 p.m. slump.

#6846. Kendall Jenner Recreated a Cult-Favorite ’80s Movie Look for Halloween

With a November 3 birthday, Kendall Jenner started the celebrations early. The model threw a party on October 30 to fête Halloween and turning 31.

On October 31, she shared photos of her costume. The model transformed into Jane Henderson, the character played by Nastassja Kinski in Wim Wenders’s 1984 film Paris, Texas. The model traded her signature brunette hair for a chin-length platinum-blonde bob. She paired the look with a fuzzy, low-back hot pink minidress and sheer black tights. In one photo, she channels a memorable scene from the film, posing at a bar.

For those unfamiliar with the reference, Paris, Texas follows Travis Henderson, a mysterious drifter who reappears after four years away and attempts to reconnect with his young son and estranged wife, Jane. Directed by Wenders and written by Pulitzer Prize-winning playwright Sam Shepard, the film is considered one of the German filmmaker’s most celebrated works. It took home the Palme d’Or at the 1984 Cannes Film Festival and has since earned cult film status. More than four decades later, its imagery remains recognizable to cinephiles and fashion fans alike.

Jenner wasn’t alone in her cinematic costume. Actor Jacob Elordi joined her as Travis Henderson, the character portrayed by Harry Dean Stanton in the film.

While it marks the rumored couple’s first costume together, it’s not the first year Jenner has thrown a birthday party during Halloween weekend. In 2019, she teased that she was “keeping the Halloween birthday party alive for mom” on Instagram.

This year’s bash, which was thrown at Bar Marmont in Los Angeles, had a star-studded guest list. Attendees included Charli XCX, Hailey Bieber, Laura Harrier, and more.

#6845. How One Mistake Unleashed a Horde of Night of the Living Dead Bootlegs

A simple copyright error allowed George A. Romero’s zombie classic to enter the public domain. For years, the filmmakers fought to get the rights back.

would you rather accidentally set your arm on fire with spilled gasoline or spend decades grappling with a paperwork mistake? Gary Streiner, who personally experienced both while making 1968’s Night of the Living Dead, says there’s no contest: “Oh, believe me, I would take the burn again.”

Streiner was the sound man on George A. Romero’s classic indie zombie flick, but since everyone on the makeshift Pittsburgh project did multiple jobs, he also helped create visual effects, like the fireball on a porch chair that would later engulf his limb. His brother, Russ Streiner, was another multitasker, playing the taunting brother Johnny in the movie’s opening, and serving as jack-of-all-trades co-producer. Both were loyal Romero confidantes who helped him found Image Ten, the production company that created the apocalyptic undead tale.

So both were among those bitten in the ass when Night of the Living Dead accidentally ended up in the public domain due to a copyright mistake that was anything but simple to fix. Even today, it hasn’t entirely been resolved, which is why there is a right way and a wrong way to watch the movie (at least, if you care about seeing it in the best possible format, while also not picking the pocket of the people who made it.)

Today, Romero is gone, and his debut feature is considered an all-time American classic. Stephen King himself calls it the scariest movie he’s ever seen. But the independent film garnered such success partly because it was so widely and freely shared. The friends and colleagues who helped Romero make Night of the Living Dead have finally figured out a way to pull the movie back into their legal possession. But the freebie versions are still out there, so they are hoping fans will be honest and honorable about the version of the film they choose to play this Halloween.

Don’t worry, they aren’t “coming to get you, Barbara,” as Russ intones in the iconic opening of the movie. They are just, once again, asking nicely.

Basically, look for the Criterion Collection logo. The beloved cinema preservation group helped the Streiners restore the original black-and-white negative for the Museum of Modern Art, and by refining the moody imagery as Romero originally intended, they technically created a “new” work that Image Ten could officially claim as their own. You can find it on the Criterion streaming service, but also HBO Max is among those that license their version of the movie.

“All of it’s licensed now. Now we get paid for it. That’s the point, every time it’s screened on any of those platforms,” Gary says.

“There are other streaming channels that aren’t,” adds Russ. If you don’t spot that big, silver C when Night of the Living Dead starts playing, you’re probably watching a bootleg.

The average joe might not care much whether these aging producers or Romero’s estate get their share, but keep in mind that these were average joes too, not Hollywood big shots. They were working stiffs who had a dream, made something immortal (in many senses of the word), and then got stiffed for real.

“To be honest with you,” Russ begins. “George—and I want to measure my words pretty carefully …”

“He was not a great businessman,” Gary cuts in, not measuring his words at all.

“None of us were great businessmen,” Russ adds with a sigh.

The Streiner brothers were toiling away on local Western Pennsylvania commercials when they helped Romero bring his feature film dream together, and their collective work inspired generations of other nobodies to max out their credit cards, load up on cameras and lights, and make a crazy movie with their friends. “It’s fair to say that without George A. Romero, I would not have the career I have now,” Shaun of the Dead and Hot Fuzz filmmaker Edgar Wright wrote when Romero died in July 2017. “A lot of people owe George a huge debt of gratitude for the inspiration. I am just one of many.”

It’s easy enough to find the right version of Night of the Living Dead, and it happens to be the one that looks the best too. Many of the bootlegs are copies of copies of copies. When the Streiners got MoMA and Criterion to revive a new version of the movie, they went back to the original negatives, part of which they possessed, and some of which belonged to Romero’s cowriter. “We gathered up all of the elements from Russ’s basement and John Russo’s garage, and we trekked out to their facility, and we were all quite amazed at the good condition that everything was still in, not having been kept in absolute ideal conditions,” Gary says.

Now it looks as good as it did when the very first prints were sent out into the world. That also happened to be the moment everything went wrong.

You know how old movies have a noticeable copyright tag in their opening credits, usually right under the title? Today, that’s an affectation, used by filmmakers like Quentin Tarantino and Rian Johnson to conjure a feeling of nostalgia. But once upon a time in real Hollywood, that was actually a legal requirement to establish ownership of a film.

Romero and his team understood this, and had it on their initial cut of the movie. The mix-up happened when they were negotiating to get the zombie picture into theaters and struck an agreement with the indie distribution company the Walter Reade Organization. “That was the beginning of our nightmare,” Russ says. “The movie at that time was called Night of the Flesh-Eaters. They didn’t like the title. They said, ‘We’ve got to come up with something new.’ ”

The executives had a point: Night of the Living Dead has a certain ring to it, so Romero and the Streiners agreed. They left the print in Walter Reade’s hands. “They were in charge of changing the title, but when the title got changed, the copyright was left off,” Russ says. “It became their responsibility to deal with their lab, and the copyright notice for Night of the Living Dead went by the wayside.”

No one realized the mistake until the prints were shipped out, and by that point it was too late. “Not that ownership was ever denied,” Gary says. “There’s never been any, ‘Well, no, you didn’t make Night of the Living Dead.’ We made it.”

Under the United States’ 1909 copyright law, this technical mistake just meant no one was legally obligated to account for tickets or pay them a portion of the sale. “That was the start of it, and we’ve been spending tons of money legally to get this thing on its right feet,” Gary adds.

If that seems both unbelievable and unfair, then welcome to the copyright law revision of the mid-1970s that eliminated this requirement. “It’s totally different now,” says Russ. “As a matter of fact, Night of the Living Dead was one of the reasons that the language for the copyright office changed, because it was preposterous.”

But it was too late for the zombie film and the team behind Image Ten, until the new restoration came about from MoMA and the Criterion Collection. The Streiners learned this lesson the hard way, but they were used to that.

Who could have guessed that a simple line of text could cause so much trouble? Well … who could have guessed that when you pour gasoline on a chair for a zombie attack scene that it could somehow also go wrong.

“George didn’t like the way it was looking, and it took about a half hour for him to reset and get the shot that he wanted,” Russ recalls of the infamous incident. “So the chair was put out, and when it came time to do another take in the new camera position, I had been all over that thing diligently making sure that there were no embers or whatever.”

He didn’t think about what was below the surface. “There was no fire on the chair itself, but the interior workings of the chair, the springs and the metal framework, were still hot,” Russ adds. “And when Gary started dousing it with gasoline—BOOM!—it was like an explosion that set him on fire.”

“I’m literally pouring gasoline out of a plastic jug, and the flame shot right up,” Gary says, mimicking the motion.

Spilled gas on his sleeve meant his whole arm was engulfed. “Fortunately, Bill Hinzman, who was a cemetery ghoul, was actually a volunteer fireman, and he had enough common sense to just tackle me and roll me to the ground,” Gary says.

It was over in seconds, with only relatively minor burns that eventually healed. The copyright conflagration took a lot longer to resolve, but now, finally, they don’t feel so scorched by it anymore.
